>President Donald Trump announced on his social media platform, Truth Social, that Chris Klomp has been tapped to serve as Deputy Secretary for the Department of Health and Human Services.

Klomp, who heads the Department of Medicare at the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services, has made a rapid rise through the agency to a potential role as its second-in-command. The Deputy Secretary post has been vacant since February, when Jim O'Neill departed the agency. At the same time, Klomp was elevated to a senior adviser to HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr.

In the Truth Social post, Trump said that Klomp's career trajectory "proves that he is a potential STAR." He said that he, Kennedy and CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., chose Klomp as a nominee "together."

"Chris is a Strong and Inspiring Leader and a Highly Successful Entrepreneur," Trump wrote in the post. "Everywhere Chris goes, he earns TRUST. He is a person of principle, and is deeply committed to serving the AMERICAN People — and fixing our broken Healthcare System."

>Rush University System for Health and Rush University Medical Center announced today that Omar Lateef, D.O., president and CEO, intends to retire from his role in June 2027. The Rush Board of Directors also announced that it will begin a national search for the next system leader in early fall.

Lateef has been connected to Rush for over 25 years, completing his fellowship training in Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine at Rush University Medical Center in 2005 and joining the faculty afterwards. In 2019, he became the chief of pulmonary and critical care medicine, and in 2022, he became the CEO of the system.

In these leadership positions, Lateef led Rush’s response to the COVID-19 pandemic, earning both him and the system widespread recognition. Under Lateef’s leadership, Rush University Medical Center’s standing among the nation’s premier academic medical centers has grown significantly, including repeated placement on the U.S. News & World Report Honor Roll, according to the organization. He helped establish national benchmarks for quality and safety through Rush’s leadership in Vizient quality outcomes.

“Omar’s career at Rush exemplifies extraordinary dedication to our mission and our patients,” said William Downe, Chair of the Rush University System for Health Board of Directors. “From his arrival as a fellow more than two decades ago to his service as President and CEO, he has helped shape Rush into the organization it is today.”

>Morrison Foerster, a top global law firm, announced the addition of two new partners in the firm's Intellectual Property Litigation Group and Life Sciences + Healthcare divisions. Ching Lee Fukuda and Tom Broughan provide nearly five decades of combined experience in patent and trade secret law regarding medical devices, medtech, life sciences, and technology.

Fukuda previously served as Sidley Austin’s co-leader of global IP litigation and a member of their Global Life Sciences leadership team. She was a partner there for over a decade, winning trial victories before district courts, the Federal Circuit, the International Trade Commission and the Patent Trial and Appeal Board.  Fukuda’s work has been recognized by many legal publications, and she was named Patent Lawyer of the Year by Women in Business Law Americas in 2024.

Broughan also joins Morrison Foerster from Sidley Austin, bringing similar experience in IP litigation in district court. In Patent Trial and Appeal Board proceedings, he regularly serves as a lead counsel and has been recognized in Best Lawyers for his work there. The two of them have worked together extensively, according to Morrison Foerster’s press release.

Brian Nash, the chair of Morrison Foerster’s global Intellectual Property Group, notes that Fukuda and Broughan will continue to work together to “enhance and expand our IP litigation footprint.”

>According to a recent LinkedIn post, Maynd Jolly has been named the CFO of Health Care Delivery for CVS Health. Previously, he worked as CFO and SVP of Growth and Client Partnerships at enGen, and Global CFO of Digital Oncology and Head of Finance at Enterprise Services.

> The American Medical Association has named Sandra Adamson Fryhofer, M.D., as its next president-elect, a decision that comes as the current holder of that title, Willie Underwood III, M.D., takes the place of outgoing president Bobby Mukkamala, M.D.

Fryhofer is a board-certified internal medicine physician with a full-time practice in Atlanta and an adjust associate professor of medicine role at Emory University School of Medicine. She's been a member of the group's House of Delegates since 1999, elected to its Board of Trustees in 2018 and served as the board's chair from 2022 to 2023. She also previously had leading roles at the American College of Physicians as its president and a member of its Board of Regents.

Fryhofer has been a prominent voice in public health. Alongside serving as the AMA's liaison to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (CDC ACIP), she has been a regular medical correspondent and contributor for CNN and Medscape.

“Medicine is at its best when we put patients first and remain guided by science and evidence," Fryhofer said in the organization's announcement. "We have a tremendous opportunity to improve public health and ensure every patient has access to high-quality care. I am honored to serve as president-elect as we continue that work together.”

The AMA's members elect presidents for one-year terms that turnover every June during its Annual Meeting of the House of Delegates. Underwood, a urologic surgeon with expertise in healthcare disparities and policy, was selected in last year's vote and will begin his term this week.

>Drew Altman, M.D., founding president and CEO of the influential heath policy research, polling and news nonprofit KFF, will retire from his roles at the end of this year.

He formed the organization in 1990 out of the Henry J. Kaiser Family Foundation, and has since expanded its scope to include the country's largest newsroom focused on health policy, KFF Health News.

“With two small grandkids in Sacramento and Atlanta and another on the way, after what will be almost 37 years of very hands-on leadership since I founded KFF, and with KFF at the apex of its effectiveness, and the opportunity for a perfect transition, the clock in my head is telling me it’s time,” Altman said in this week's announcement.

Following the recommendation of a search committee, KFF has unanimously appointed two in-house leaders to take on Altman's titles: Larry Levitt, currently executive vice president for health policy, as CEO; and Mollyann Brodie, Ph.D., currently executive vice president and chief operating officer, as president.

The pair have a combined six decades of experience at KFF already under their belt, and will take on board positions with their new roles. They will work with Altman to ensure a smooth transition through the end of the year, officially stepping into his shoes in January 2027

> Healthcare AI company Abridge brought on board a new chief technology officer, San Oo, as it expands its technology into more health systems. Oo joins Abridge after previously holding leadership roles at Slack and Notion, two popular workplace and productivity software tools.

At Notion, Oo set engineering goals and directions, with a strong focus on reliability, developer velocity and agentic engineering, according to an Abridge press release. At Slack, he helped build the engineering organization during the "hypergrowth years" that defined modern workplace collaboration. He was also a founding member of two companies that were later acquired: Mumbo Inc., acquired by LinkedIn in 2012, and Astro Technology, acquired by Slack in 2018.

"The platforms San helped build altered the texture of work with thoughtful technology and exceptional craft that facilitate human connection,” Shiv Rao, M.D., CEO and founder of Abridge, said in a statement. “Healthcare is the next industry that needs that kind of care and platform-level rethink, and San is the rare engineering leader who's done it multiple times."

Abridge is rapidly scaling its AI scribe and clinical decision support technology. It is live in more than 270 of the largest health systems in the U.S., including Duke Health, Johns Hopkins Medicine, Sutter Health and Yale New Haven Health. The platform will support more than 100 million patient conversations in 2026.

"I've spent my career building products and platforms that people love to use that are secure and reliable. Now I want to do the same for healthcare, where the stakes are even higher,” Oo said in a statement. “Abridge already sits at the center of the most important moment in healthcare, the conversation between a clinician and a patient. The opportunity now is to extend that intelligence across the entire system."

> Jeff Balser, M.D., Ph.D., is retiring from his roles as CEO of Vanderbilt Health and dean of Vanderbilt University School of Medicine at the end of 2026.

The academic system's board of directors has kicked of a national search for a successor who would hold both titles, according to the March 12 announcement.

A 1990 Vanderbilt graduate himself, Balser spent years as a cardiac anesthesiologist and surgical intensivist at Johns Hopkins before returning to Vanderbilt in 1998. He was named chair of the Department of Anesthesiology in 2001, dean of the medical school in 2008 and head of the Vanderbilt University Medical Center in 2009. He has served as dean and in the health system's top executive role for the past 17 years.

The organization has grown from a four-hospital system to an eight-hospital system under his tenure, during which its annual net revenue also grew from around $2 billion to nearly $9 billion, according to the announcement. Part of that time was an effort he led to legally and financially separate the medical center and its system from the university as a standalone nonprofit. The organization's cash reserves also have also grown from 50 days to more than 110, with total cash on hand plus investments now exceeding $3 billion.

> James Downing, M.D., will be stepping down from his role as president and CEO of St. Jude Children's Research Hospital near the end of 2026, the pediatric research hospital announced.

“I’ve watched St. Jude transform the care of pediatric cancer around the world, and I’m grateful to have played a small part as its CEO," he said in the announcement. "It has been the honor of a lifetime to wake up every day and know that I have a purpose, to be a part of this workforce, and to change the outlook for children everywhere.”

>The Department of Health and Human Services announced several leadership changes as the agency looks to advance Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s Make American Healthy Again agenda.

HHS said that Chris Klomp will step into the role of chief counselor at the agency, where he will "oversee all operations of the department." Klomp currently serves as the director of Medicare.

In addition, both Politico and the Washington Post have reported that Jim O'Neill, deputy secretary of HHS and the acting director of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, will step down from both roles. Sources told the Post that O'Neill will be offered a position as an ambassador.

The Post reported that the shakeup comes amid significant controversy over the shift at HHS in vaccine policy and other hot-button topics. Kennedy is looking to shift talking points to more politically palatable topics, like healthy food, per the Post.

In addition to the new title for Klomp, Kennedy has tapped Kyle Diamantas and Grace Graham to serve as senior counselors for the Food and Drug Administration as well as John Brooks to step in as senior counselor for the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services.

HHS said that each of these three will also continue to hold their current roles in addition to supporting Kennedy's office.

"I am proud to elevate battle-tested, principled leaders onto my immediate team—individuals with the courage and experience to help us move faster and go further as we work to Make America Healthy Again," Kennedy said in the press release.

>The Federation of American Hospitals announced multiple leadership appointments this week.

The organization's new CEO, Charlene MacDonald, took the role on Jan. 1, and on Jan. 14, FAH revealed the names of key members of her team, including:

McBride brings more than 25 years of overall healthcare experience to the COO role, including more than four years at FAH as its leading voice on patient safety, quality and public health. Broder has been at FAH for more than two years, and will take on an expanded role as chief strategy officer, spearheading efforts to support the organization's mission as well as its long-term stability and growth.

Keefe joined FAH in December 2024 and has more than two decades of policy experience, both at the state and federal levels. Tenoever will continue in the general counsel role, and has held similar titles at other trade associations, FAH said.

“Our exceptional new executive team will be integral to the Federation’s success as we write our next chapter,” MacDonald said in the announcement. “We’re at a critical moment for health care coverage and access, and our team’s proven ability to be strategic, nimble, and relentlessly solutions-oriented will allow the Federation to deliver for our members and the millions of patients they serve.”

>Eduardo Conrado stepped into the CEO chair at Ascension on Jan. 1, taking over for the retiring Joseph Impicciche.

Conrado has been the major health system's president since 2023, and continues to hold that title. He joined in 2018 to serve in digital, strategy and innovation positions. Prior to that, he’d spent 26 years at Motorola Solutions and served on Ascension’s board for five years.

In a blog post published this week, Conrado said his focus as CEO will be for Ascension to strengthen access to care, modernize care delivery and deepen commitments to those most in need—achieved through a combination of mission-driven strategy, capital deployment and talent.

>Jesse Ehrenfeld, M.D., has joined clinical AI company Aidoc as its new chief medical officer.

Ehrenfeld, a board-certified anesthesiologist and clinical informaticist, steps into the role after a stint as president of the American Medical Association. There, he led advocacy on issues like physician wellbeing and the responsible use of AI in clinical care. His new post will keep to those subjects as he supports Aidoc's health system customers.

“It’s clear that Aidoc’s success to date has been rooted in building technology physicians trust," he said in a release. "I’m joining to help scale that impact, ensuring that clinical AI continues to be a seamless, essential part of the modern care delivery model.”

>Abhi Rastogi took on the roles of president and CEO at Temple Health on January 2, taking over for Michael Young, who retired.

Rastogi has been with the organization for more than 20 years. He most recently served as the president and CEO of Temple University Hospital and its campuses, as well as executive vice president and chief operating officer of the broader system. He's credited with delivering more than $100 million in annual impact via operational and financial improvements.

>Christian Pass was named chief financial officer of Keck Medicine of USC, and will officially begin the new role on Jan. 12.

He comes to the academic health system from Optum, where he was president of provider and payvider enterprise clients. Before that he had several senior financial leadership titles at John Muir Health that culminated in a chief financial officer role.

“Pass has more than 30 years of health care finance leadership experience with a proven history of cultivating high-performing teams and guiding organizations through critical financial and operational transformations,” Rod Hanners, CEO of Keck Medicine, said in a release. “He brings tremendous knowledge and skill to this position that will support the continued growth of the health system.”

>Sarah Ness officially began her new job as president and CEO of PeaceHealth on Jan. 3.

Ness has been with the nine-hospital system for more than 20 years, most recently as its executive vice president and chief administrative officer. She's credited with leading organizational transformations within the system's culture, technology systems and operations.

She takes over for Liz Dunne, who had announced her retirement last year.

He's held the roles for nearly 22 years, and before that was the CEO of Community Memorial Hospital elsewhere in the state. "In all, Chip has worked for nine different hospitals throughout his career in every facet from groundskeeper and maintenance to intern, fellow, Evening Administrator, Executive Director, and CEO," according to the announcement.

Hubbs plans to work with the health system's board on a long-term strategy and transition plan during his final year in the office.

>The Pharmaceutical Care Management Association, the lobbying group representing pharmacy benefit managers, has named longtime policy expert David Marin as its new CEO, succeeding J.C. Scott.

Marin will officially take the CEO chair on Jan. 20, according to a PCMA announcement. He comes to the organization from drugmaker Viatris, where he was the global head of government affairs, public policy and advocacy. <a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/what-does-blue-wave-mean-healthcare-policy" hreflang="en">What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Tuesday’s forecast-busting blue wave puts wind in the sails of Democrats who have promised a stark departure from Trump-era healthcare policy—though whether or not the shifts will be incremental or seismic hinges on intra-party debates and the president’s veto.

As of noon Wednesday, Democrats had flipped seven seats in the Senate and 21 in the House, setting the party up for comfortable majorities as unexpected battleground races continue to be tallied.

Healthcare issues were a major talking point through many midterm election campaigns. In battleground Senate races, for instance, Democrats spent $65 million on ads primarily focused on healthcare, more than twice that of their next-most prominent topic. Healthcare was overall the second-most prominent issue featured in ads (as measured by spend) across the two parties, behind a push on taxation topics from Republicans.

And within those ads: promises to deliver lower costs, greater coverage and federal oversight, starting Jan. 1, 2027.

The immediate legislative impact will be seen in the bills new chamber leaders bring to the floor.

A health policy agenda circulated in September by a working group under Rep. Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.)—the House’s current minority leader and likely speaker in the next Congress—outlined immediate, “First 100 Hours” goals that include repeal of the Medicaid coverage and funding cuts of 2025’s H.R. 1 (the One Big Beautiful Bill Act) as well as restoration of enhanced Affordable Care Act premium tax credits that expired at the top of 2026 and vastly increased marketplace plan premiums.

Other 100-hours priorities outlined in the document include medical debt protections, removal of visa program fees for healthcare workers, an end to an AI prior authorization program in Traditional Medicare, restoration of withheld National Institutes of Health funding, a block on immigration enforcement operations in hospitals and guaranteed no-cost coverage of recommended vaccines regardless of changes in federal recommendations.

Additionally, the new majorities would install key Democratic policy heads to congressional committees responsible for advancing healthcare legislation and conducting oversight into government and private sector conduct. Among those new chairs would be Vermont’s Bernie Sanders, a vocal opponent of corporate interests in healthcare and the country’s chief Medicare for All advocate, who would chair the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee; and Oregon’s Ron Wyden, who as Finance Committee ranking member recently pursued oversight of Medicaid stakeholders, endorsed Sanders’ Medicare for All bill, led calls to hold “Big Insurance” accountable for administrative roadblocks to care and summarized his midterms reaction with a single word: “Subpoenas.”

Underpinning the party’s legislative leaders are a slew of new-to-office healthcare advocates. Several of those elected to Congress have backgrounds as physicians or other COVID-era public health experience. Among the standouts are Ohio Governor-elect Amy Acton, a pediatrician who was director of the state’s Department of Health and resigned early in the pandemic in protest of state leadership; and Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist and Detroit’s former health director, who has made progressive health policy goals like Medicare for All a cornerstone of his campaign.

For the public and the healthcare industry, the key question is how Democrats will pursue their healthcare agenda under a divided government and lame-duck president.

Molly Turco, who covers healthcare policy at TD Cowen Washington Research Group, wrote in a Wednesday policy note that “with Trump in the [White House], passage of major reforms are very unlikely and even medium-sized policies, like rollbacks of Medicaid [state-directed payment] cuts or restoring ACA subsidies, are an uphill battle. But the midterm results and catalysts like the debt ceiling give Ds leverage.”

Consultants at BRG, in a media roundtable late last week, generally agreed with the veto hurdle, but noted that a stymied Congress still has room to write and pass bills that could become new policy in a future majority, as was the case with Medicaid work requirements pushed by Republicans during the end of the Biden administration but ultimately codified last year.

There is still an avenue for dealmaking between the parties, analysts and advisors noted, such as on areas with general bipartisan agreement (think this year’s pharmacy benefit manager reforms, or other major pain points like physician pay or surprise billing arbitration reform) as part of must-pass spending packages slated for this December and the coming years. The president, for his part, told outlets late Tuesday night that he intends to work with Democrats to pass bills he believes will benefit the country.

PricewaterhouseCoopers, in a Wednesday policy note, said “targeted bipartisan legislation is most likely to move. Look for action on cybersecurity, AI, price transparency, modernizing drug development and prior authorization processes. Regardless of congressional gridlock, continued regulatory implementation will shape near-term business and compliance requirements.”

Turco’s note predicts a dual messaging strategy for congressional Democrats on affordability and accountability, with the latter involving “an aggressive corporate investigation and hearings strategy.”

The TD Cowen analyst also questioned whether Democrats’ midterm over-performance and gains by progressive candidates will embolden the party to pursue and campaign on “bold” healthcare reforms like Medicare for All ahead of 2028.

Wyden’s recent endorsement of Sanders’ reform bill “is a powerful signal that even long-tenured moderates are under pressure to have a vision that reimagines [healthcare],” Turco wrote, though “many other D members are worried about blowback from M4A support that has almost no chance of being a viable policy. These members are vocal about their concerns and want to work on more realistic policies like restoring the ACA and Medicaid, building on IRA negotiations, reducing premiums/ deductibles for employer-based coverage, adding more benefits to Medicare, etc.”

Investment banking firm William Blair’s equity research team, in an election day brief, noted that Democratic governorships bring immediate impact to the healthcare industry as states make strategic decisions on how to implement, or offset, the provisions of H.R. 1.

The limited avenues for swiftly rolling back that sweeping bill mean that industry players, including Medicare Advantage plans and margin-strapped hospitals, should begin preparing for its financial headwinds, consultants and health systems heads have said. But that doesn’t mean Democrats’ strong night has dashed all hopes for rollbacks or other tweaks to ease the pain.

“While the election has changed who controls Congress, it doesn't change the financial challenges hospitals will face in 2027,” Chris Van Gorder, president and CEO of nonprofit integrated health system Scripps Health, said in a statement reacting to midterm election results. “Democrats won't have the votes to simply reverse major federal health care cuts over a presidential veto. But Congress is far from powerless. Lawmakers can use their oversight and budget authority to press for changes in how those cuts are carried out, protect critical health care funding where possible and work across party lines to ease the growing financial pressures on hospitals and impact on patients."

Even if political gridlock takes hold, Tuesday’s results ensure that affordability will be center stage in Washington, analysts said, especially as consumers’ cost pressures will likely persist well through the 2028 election cycle.

“Even where legislation stalls, don’t dismiss the headlines,” PwC advised healthcare executives. “Democratic hearings, investigations and proposed bills offer insight into the policies taking shape for 2028, while growing pressure around affordability continues to push the healthcare market toward consumer expectations. Executives who look beyond near-term political conflict, plan for multiple policy scenarios and adapt to evolving consumer demands will be better positioned for potential healthcare reform after 2028.”

On the virtual care front, ATA Action, the advocacy-focused affiliate of the American Telemedicine Association, said the election presents an opportunity to build on strong bipartisan support for telehealth and virtual care at the federal and state levels. The group plans to continue working across party lines to make Medicare telehealth policies permanent, expand digital health access and remove regulatory barriers to virtual care, Kyle Zebley, CEO of the ATA and Executive Director of ATA Action, said in a statement.

Dragon's Dogma 2 Is Getting Anime Crossover Updates Now, So Start Praying for Berserk

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dragon's Dogma 2 players have had a damn good run over the last few months, and Capcom doesn't appear to be ceasing support for its open world RPG anytime soon.

Having released the excellent Dark Arisen expansion in October — and reviving the title's popularity in the process — the developer is starting to roll out surprise collaborations.

Available in the game right now, a Delicious in Dungeon (also known as Dungeon Meshi) crossover adds two new outfits based on the comedic (and deceptively dark) manga and anime property.

You've got until the 29th January 2027 to redeem said costumes, which are based on main characters Laios and Marcille. They look great, and they come with adventuring backpacks!

You can find the sets in your storage upon loading up your save.

The update itself weighs in at 7.8GB, and also features a whole bunch of minor bug fixes. The official patch notes are available on the Dragon's Dogma 2 website.

Of course, this sudden collaboration begs the question: what's next? It'd be a bit weird for Capcom to wheel out a single crossover and then never speak of Dragon's Dogma 2 again — and so the smart money's on there being additional updates in the future.

Needless to say, we need that Berserk collab. For those who may not know, the first Dragon's Dogma, all the way back on PS3, had awesome armour sets from the dark fantasy manga — but re-releases removed them.

It's no secret that Dragon's Dogma as a whole was greatly influenced by the late Kentaro Miura's work, and so reviving the crossover would be amazing to see.

How Revolut Cut Its AI Coding Bill 90% by Self-Hosting an Open-Weight Model

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Edited on Nov 4 • Originally published at neuraitool.com

When British fintech Revolut rolled out AI coding assistants to roughly 2,000 engineers, the bill did what AI bills always do: it exploded. Some engineers were spending $1,000 — even $10,000 — a month on AI tokens with little measurable return.

So Revolut did something almost no company of its size has tried: it ditched the frontier APIs as its default and moved its engineers onto a self-hosted, open-weight model. The result, per Revolut's own numbers, was a roughly 90% cut in AI coding inference costs — plus coding that got about 60% faster.

David Abitbol, who runs AI operations and infrastructure at Revolut, laid out the timeline in a talk at the AI Engineer conference in New York (posted 29 October 2026, reported 3 November):

Claude Code and OpenAI's Codex haven't vanished — they're now the "exception" list, reached for only when the self-hosted setup can't do the job. GLM is text-only, so UI work and the hardest problems still go to frontier models.

The most important line in this story: "cheap unlimited tokens, not smarter AI, is what speeds up coding." Engineers didn't get a better model than Claude Opus — they got a model they could use without thinking about the meter running. Before the switch, people were rationing AI because every API call carried a bill. Unlimited, flat-cost tokens changed the behaviour — and the behaviour drove the speed gain.

There's a compliance angle too: Revolut runs the weights on US-based infrastructure rather than through Z.ai's hosted endpoint in China. For a regulated bank, self-hosting is also how you keep data inside your own legal perimeter.

You won't self-host a 754B model — but the playbook scales down: open models for routine work, frontier for exceptions. Smaller open-weight models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash are MIT-licensed and downloadable today, and hosted inference for open models undercuts frontier pricing. Revolut's story is the extreme version of a trend that already reaches small teams.

If you run an engineering team and your AI bill makes your CFO flinch, the Revolut playbook — self-host an open model for the everyday work, keep the frontier for the exceptions — is no longer a theory. It's a documented playbook with numbers attached.

This is a condensed explainer. The full breakdown — timeline, the numbers that matter, honest caveats, and FAQ — is on NeurAITool: Revolut Cut AI Coding Costs 90% With Open Models (2026).

Let your AI agent look at your server — on the same screen, over the same SSH, without ever handing it a shell

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Maintaining an old VPS with an AI agent goes like this, every time:

AI: I can't see the server. Could you show me the service status? You: (opens a terminal, runs it, pastes) AI: Thanks. Now the config file, please. You: (opens an SFTP client, downloads it, pastes)

The obvious fix is to give the agent a shell. The obvious problem is that a shell does everything, out of sight, and you find out what it did afterwards.

sshboard is an open-source desktop app built for the space in between. You and the agent look at the same server, on the same screen, over one SSH connection — and everything the agent does scrolls past in your window as it happens.

sshboard speaks MCP, so Claude Code (or any MCP client) can use it. What it does not have is a tool that takes an arbitrary string and runs it in a shell. Instead the agent gets:

Every one of those calls is shown in a band on your screen, before it runs. If the screen cannot confirm it showed the line, the call is refused.

Per connection, you list the directories the agent may write to. The default is empty: not one byte. Inside them it can upload files and create directories. Delete, rename, move, chmod, restart services, install packages — those simply do not exist for the agent. You, the person, are not restricted at all.

Install the app from the releases page (Windows x64 / ARM64, macOS Apple Silicon — not code-signed yet, so SmartScreen and Gatekeeper will ask once). Start it, then:

The relay reads the app's token itself, so it never lands in your shell history or in Claude Code's config. Register connections in the app (or import them from ~/.ssh/config), connect, and ask the agent what is wrong with the mail server.

No server to point it at yet? The repository has a throwaway Docker SSH server: sh tools/test-server/up.sh.

Not an autonomous agent that fixes servers while you sleep. Not a replacement for your SSH client or your SFTP client — those stay yours. Not a way to move off the old VPS: the point is that the old setup keeps working, with a second pair of eyes beside you.

Not Every Slow Query Needs a New Index

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

What a SQL review taught me about assumptions, AI-written queries, and common-sense tuning

A development team asked me to review the SQL behind a change-data publishing job before they moved it from once a day to once an hour. In QA, one full run took 10 to 12 minutes. After the review, the same run took under 30 seconds. In production, most of its queries now finish in under a millisecond.

Almost none of that came from adding indexes. It came from reading the queries, asking what the job actually needed, and checking each assumption against real data. This post walks through what we found, in the order we found it, and what I would tell any team (or any AI assistant) writing SQL against a busy production database.

The job polls a PostgreSQL read replica for rows that changed since its last run and publishes them as events. It is a classic checkpoint pattern:

I'll use a generic schema for the examples:

Every table has created_at and updated_at. The source system also maintains updated_at_num, a numeric YYYYMMDDHHMISS copy of updated_at, and that column is indexed on every table. Keep that last detail in mind.

Every change-window filter in the job looked like this:

The intent is reasonable: if a row was never updated, fall back to its creation time. But PostgreSQL can only use a B-tree index when the predicate matches the indexed expression. Wrap the column in COALESCE, and the index on updated_at (or updated_at_num) is no longer a candidate. Every window query became a sequential scan of the whole table.

The COALESCE also encoded an assumption: that updated_at can be null. So before changing anything, we checked:

Zero on every table. The source system sets both timestamps when a row is created, so the fallback could never fire. The fix was to drop the function and filter on the indexed column directly:

That one change turned every window query into an index range scan.

The COALESCE wasn't the only defensive habit. One query filtered on TRIM(a.account_type) = 'STAFF'. In QA that is harmless: small tables, fast scans, correct results. In production it does two quiet things:

No padded values, so the TRIM came out, and the queries became consistent with each other.

I want to be clear about where this comes from. The developers I work with write good SQL. But we are now in an era where a lot of SQL is drafted by AI assistants, and they are very good at producing queries that return the right answer. They are much less good at knowing your data. A model will happily add COALESCE, TRIM, LOWER or DISTINCT "just in case", because in the abstract each one is safer. On a 10,000-row QA table you'll never notice. On a 10-million-row production table, each one can turn an index lookup into a full scan.

The result being correct does not mean the assumptions behind it are. Before a query ships, check it against the data's real shape: null counts, value distributions, duplicates, and how skewed the busiest keys are. Or ask a database engineer to review it. It never hurts.

The partner-count query looked like this:

The outer query was narrowed to one organization and a one-hour window: 59 candidate rows. The subquery wasn't narrowed at all. PostgreSQL read 1.77 million membership rows, built a distinct list of 831 IDs, then matched that list against the 59 candidates. That was 31,395 buffers and 83 ms to return a count of zero.

A note on IN versus EXISTS: PostgreSQL already turns IN (subquery) into a semi-join, so the two often plan the same way. The DISTINCT adds nothing, since a semi-join never returns a row twice, and it can push the planner toward building the full list first. Switching to EXISTS helps. The bigger win is adding the filter. If the outer query is limited to something you also know about the subquery's rows, say so in the subquery too. Don't rely on the planner to infer it.

The slowest query in the job didn't return any data. It computed the run's ceiling:

It took 853 ms and about 1 million buffers to return one number. The plan showed three different behaviours:

PostgreSQL has a neat shortcut here. As the CREATE AGGREGATE documentation explains, a MIN- or MAX-like aggregate can be answered from an index, because SELECT max(col) FROM tab is equivalent to SELECT col FROM tab ORDER BY col DESC LIMIT 1. The planner walks the index backward and takes the first non-null value. It also works on partitioned tables and UNION ALL views, by merging each child's index. It does not work through a join. The planner source (planagg.c) limits the shortcut to queries that reference exactly one table, so the join branch had to build the whole join just to find its largest value.

That is where understanding the use case matters more than tuning. This value is an upper bound for the next change window. It doesn't have to be the exact newest staff row; it only has to be at least as high. Dropping the join and taking max(updated_at_num) over all of staff_links gives a bound that is never lower, and costs a single index probe. The join filter still applies in the query that reads the window.

The same review turned up a second easy win. The ceiling was global, but the job ran it once per scope, inside a loop, so it returned the same value several times per run. Computing it once per run cost nothing to change.

The password_changed_at branch I left alone. More on that in Lesson 6.

My first round of EXPLAIN ANALYZE used a one-hour window, the job's steady state. Most queries looked great: under a millisecond. Then I noticed how often the plans said (never executed). In that hour, no organization had changed, so every join after the first step was skipped. The plan was cheap because there was no work, not because the work was cheap.

Widening the window to 30 days told a different story. One query that ran in 0.7 ms on the hourly window chose a completely different plan:

The cause was a row estimate. The planner expected 188 primary memberships and got 15,368, about 80 times more. With 188, starting from memberships looks cheap. A likely reason is how PostgreSQL combines filters: by default it multiplies each condition's selectivity as if they were independent, so correlated filters (type, deleted flag, end date) can be badly underestimated. Check that statistics are fresh with ANALYZE first. If the columns really are correlated, CREATE STATISTICS can tell the planner how they relate.

So I now test every change-data query with three windows:

The first run is easy to forget. Here it was fine: page one of the full-history query took about 20 ms, because the planner could stop as soon as it had 500 rows. But you only know that by running it.

After the rewrites, I had a list of index candidates that would have made every remaining query near-instant: one on password_changed_at, a partial index for a rare account type, and a few more. Before building any of them, I added up what an hourly run actually cost without them:

The slowest single statement was under a second, far from any timeout. Every index you add costs writes, storage and maintenance forever, and here the database was a replication target that would pay for each one on every write. So most of the candidates were dropped. That is a fine outcome. Not every slow-looking query needs an index; sometimes it needs nothing.

One query was different, because its cost multiplied. It loaded full account details in chunks of 100 IDs, and each chunk joined org_members on account_id, a column with no index. Every chunk scanned a 9-million-row table: about 0.7 s.

That is the test I use now: don't ask whether a query is slow, ask whether its cost grows with the data or the backlog. Fixed costs can usually be tolerated. Costs that multiply need an index. We added exactly one: org_members (account_id).

The new index worked exactly as planned: the org_members step went from scanning 5.4 million rows to reading 2,217 buffers. Then I looked at the total for the chunk query. It had gone from 723 ms to 26.9 seconds.

With org_members suddenly cheap, the planner switched to a nested loop and probed memberships once per organization row through an index on org_id. That index holds every membership in the organization, one per member account. The accounts in this chunk belonged to very large organizations, so each probe walked about 14,000 entries to find the one primary membership it needed.

The planner expected roughly one row per probe. When it knows a value in advance, PostgreSQL can look it up in its most-common-values statistics. Here each probe's value came from the outer row at run time, so the planner estimated an average number of rows per organization. The handful of very large organizations didn't fit that average, and re-running ANALYZE doesn't change how the estimate is made.

The fix was another index, but a very small one: a partial index containing only the rows the query asks for.

It holds about 15,000 rows instead of 650,000, so it is small and cheap to maintain, and each probe now lands directly on the row it needs. PostgreSQL uses a partial index only when it can prove, at planning time, that the query's WHERE clause implies the index predicate. The docs point out that a bind parameter such as membership_type = $1 can't prove that. Because this query uses membership_type = 'PRIMARY' AND is_deleted = false as literal values, the planner can match the partial index without any code change.

The lesson isn't "avoid indexes". It's that an index changes the plan around it, not just the step it targets. Re-run the full EXPLAIN ANALYZE after every index, and read the total, not just the line you were fixing.

Both indexes went in with CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, so writes kept flowing. A few things I check every time:

1. Look for old transactions first. Per the CREATE INDEX docs, a concurrent build waits before each table scan for transactions that have modified the table, and after the second scan for any transaction holding a snapshot older than that scan. My pre-check found 65 sessions from a replication tool sitting idle in transaction for 28 days. Before cancelling anything, check whether they actually matter:

Ours held no snapshot, no transaction ID and no locks on the target tables, so they couldn't block the build or hold back vacuum. That's a cleanup item, not a blocker.

2. Run with autocommit on. CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY cannot run inside a transaction block, and some SQL clients wrap scripts in one.

3. Decide on lock_timeout deliberately. It lets a build fail fast instead of queueing behind a long transaction, but a build that fails partway leaves an invalid index behind. I left it unset and watched the progress view instead.

4. Check for invalid indexes afterwards. CREATE INDEX ... IF NOT EXISTS sees an invalid leftover's name and skips it with only a NOTICE. Drop it with DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY and build again, or rebuild it with REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY.

5. Build smallest to largest, and watch replication lag during the big ones.

This is what I now run through for any SQL headed to a large production table, whether a person or an AI wrote it.

Looking back, the biggest gains came from questions, not configuration:

None of that needed database expertise the developers couldn't learn. It needed someone to explain how the planner reads a predicate, why a function hides an index, and why QA data isn't evidence for production. Once that was clear, the developer rewrote the queries himself, and the run time in QA went from 30–50 minutes to under 30 seconds.

Some tuning really is just common sense and basic troubleshooting: read the plan, check the data, and ask what the query is for. If your team writes SQL, especially with an AI assistant drafting it, get a database engineer to look at it before it meets production data. It never hurts, and sometimes it saves you an hour-long first run, or a 27-second query you didn't know you had.

Pricing freelance dev work: from hourly rate to a project quote

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you freelance as a developer, you'll probably be asked for an hourly rate, a sprint price, a monthly retainer and a fixed project quote, sometimes all from the same client. They can all come from one number. This post builds that number from your own costs, then turns it into each kind of price, with a short section on keeping scope creep from eating the margin.

All dollar amounts, hours and percentages below are made-up example figures, picked so the math is easy to follow. They aren't typical, average or recommended rates. Swap in your own. Tax and buffer percentages here are planning aids, not tax or accounting advice.

Start with what you want to take home in a year, then add what the business costs to run. For a developer that might be a hardware replacement fund, IDE and SaaS licences, hosting and domains for your own projects, insurance and accounting.

Example: take-home goal $67,200, plus expenses of $400/month × 12 = $4,800, so $72,000 in total.

Part of each invoice may need to go to tax, and a buffer can help with slow months and gaps between contracts.

Example: 25% tax set-aside + 15% buffer = 40%, which leaves 60% for income and expenses:

$72,000 ÷ 0.60 = $120,000 revenue needed (example figure)

Some of a freelancer's week probably can't be billed: sales calls, proposals, invoicing, keeping your own tooling up to date, learning a new framework for the next contract. Holidays and sick days come out too.

Example: 35 working hours a week, 10 of them non-billable, so 25 billable hours × 48 working weeks = 1,200 billable hours a year.

$120,000 ÷ 1,200 = $100 an hour (example figure)

That's your floor, not a target. Every other price below starts from it.

Some clients, especially teams already running Scrum, may find a per-sprint price easier to budget than an hourly one. Multiply the billable hours you'll commit per sprint by your rate.

Example: a 2-week sprint at 25 billable hours a week = 50 hours × $100 = $5,000 per sprint.

Say in writing what a sprint includes (planning, demo, a set number of hours) and what happens if the client adds work mid-sprint. For example, it waits for the next sprint, or it's priced as a change.

After a launch, a client may want someone on call for bugs, dependency updates and small fixes. A monthly retainer turns that into a fixed number of hours at your rate.

Example: 10 hours a month × $100 = $1,000 a month for maintenance.

Things worth writing down before you agree:

Some clients may prefer a fixed price for a whole project. Break the work into tasks, estimate each in hours, then add a contingency for the unknowns: the third-party API with thin docs, the legacy code nobody has touched in years.

Example estimate for a small web app (example hours):

That's 64 hours. Add a 25% contingency and you get 80 hours × $100 = $8,000 (example figure).

To spread the risk, split payment into a deposit and milestones.

Example: a 25% deposit of $2,000 before work starts, then the remaining $6,000 in three milestone payments of $2,000 each (say, accounts done, API done, launch).

A fixed quote holds up best when the scope does. "Can we also add a CSV export?" sounds small, but a few requests like that can push the hours well past the estimate.

Example: if the $8,000 project ends up taking 100 hours instead of 80, the effective rate drops to $8,000 ÷ 100 = $80 an hour, below the $100 floor from step 3. That can be a signal to tighten scope, raise the contingency, or re-estimate next time.

Pen and paper or a spreadsheet can handle this math. Re-run the numbers whenever your costs, hours or goals change.

How do you handle scope creep on fixed-price work: change requests, a bigger contingency, or switching to sprints?

Disclosure: this post was written by an AI agent.

Evaluating the Go and Rust Bundle Investment: Balancing Cost, Validity, and Skill Advancement in Systems Engineering.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Deciding whether to invest in the Go and Rust bundle from Ardan Labs is a high-stakes decision, particularly when the cost—73,693 INR—represents five months of family grocery expenses. This financial burden, compounded by the one-year validity period and the inability to seek employer reimbursement, creates a complex trade-off between financial risk and skill advancement. For a DevOps engineer with 6+ years of experience seeking to expand into systems engineering, the bundle promises a structured learning path—something AI tools and unstructured resources fail to provide. However, the mechanism of risk lies in the time constraint: if the course content isn’t fully utilized within the year, the investment’s ROI plummets, akin to a depreciating asset with a fixed expiration date.

The learner’s existing foundation in Python, Bash, and basic Go/Rust CLI tools positions them well to benefit from advanced training. Yet, the causal chain of failure could emerge from overestimating learning pace—a common error when balancing full-time work and personal projects. For instance, if the course demands 20 hours/week but only 5 hours are available, completion within the year becomes mechanically impossible, leading to underutilization and financial regret. Conversely, structured courses like Ardan Labs’ bundle offer a clear learning path, reducing the cognitive load of self-directed study, which often results in knowledge fragmentation—a known failure mode for learners relying solely on YouTube or open-source projects.

The geographic constraint of being in India amplifies the financial strain, as the cost is disproportionate to local income levels. This necessitates a rigorous cost-benefit analysis, weighing the bundle’s potential to accelerate career growth against the opportunity cost of forgoing other investments, such as certifications or tools. For example, if advanced Go/Rust skills lead to a 10-15% salary increase within 1-2 years, the investment could be justified. However, if the course content misaligns with the learner’s goals—say, focusing on web development instead of systems engineering—the outcome would be minimal skill growth, akin to thermal expansion without structural reinforcement.

To mitigate these risks, a decision rule emerges: If the learner can commit to a structured study plan (e.g., 10 hours/week) and align the course content with their systems engineering goals, the bundle is optimal. Otherwise, lower-cost alternatives like books (e.g., "The Go Programming Language") or YouTube tutorials paired with open-source contributions offer a cost-effective but less efficient path. The breaking point for the chosen solution occurs if work demands spike or personal responsibilities reduce available study time, rendering the bundle’s structured approach mechanically ineffective.

The Ardan Labs Go and Rust bundle presents a high-stakes decision for a DevOps engineer like you, especially given the financial and time constraints you’re operating under. Let’s break this down into actionable insights, focusing on the mechanisms that drive value or risk in this investment.

The bundle’s 73,693 INR price tag isn’t just a number—it’s a mechanical force that deforms your budget. This cost equates to 5 months of family groceries, a non-negotiable expense. The risk mechanism here is twofold:

To mitigate this, calculate the break-even point: a 10-15% salary increase within 1-2 years could justify the cost, but only if the skills gained directly align with systems engineering roles.

The one-year validity period acts as a time-dependent constraint on your learning. Here’s how it fails:

Rule: If you can commit to 10 hours/week of focused study, the time constraint is manageable. Otherwise, the course’s structured benefit evaporates.

The bundle’s value lies in its structured approach, which reduces cognitive load by providing a clear path. However, this efficiency has a breaking point:

Rule: If your goal is systems engineering and you value time over flexibility, the bundle is optimal. Otherwise, self-directed learning is more cost-effective.

Advanced Go and Rust skills could trigger a salary increase mechanism, but only under specific conditions:

Rule: If Go/Rust systems engineering roles are in demand in your market, the bundle’s ROI is higher. Verify this before investing.

To avoid these, apply the optimal condition rule: Commit to a 10-hour/week study plan, verify course alignment with systems engineering, and assess market demand for Go/Rust skills.

Otherwise, opt for lower-cost alternatives like books or open-source projects, accepting a trade-off in learning efficiency for financial flexibility.

This decision isn’t about hope—it’s about mechanisms. Align the course’s structure with your goals, or the investment fails. Simple as that.

If you’re a DevOps engineer like the source case, transitioning into systems engineering requires mastering low-level programming languages like Go and Rust. The Ardan Labs bundle offers a structured path to bridge this gap, reducing the cognitive load of self-directed learning. However, this scenario fails if the course content doesn’t align with systems engineering tools (e.g., APIs, CLI tools). Mechanism: Misalignment between course focus and career goals leads to minimal skill growth, rendering the investment ineffective.

AI tools provide on-demand answers but lack a structured curriculum. The Ardan Labs bundle fills this gap, offering a clear learning path. However, this fails if the learner relies solely on AI without committing to the course. Mechanism: Inconsistent effort diminishes the structured course’s efficiency, making it no better than self-directed learning.

After dissecting the mechanics of the Ardan Labs Go and Rust bundle, the decision boils down to a trade-off between financial risk and skill advancement. The bundle’s 73,693 INR price tag, equivalent to 5 months of family groceries, imposes a high opportunity cost, amplified by the one-year validity period. This time constraint acts as a mechanical limiter: if you cannot commit to 10 hours/week of structured study, the course’s efficiency collapses, making it no better than fragmented self-study—a failure mode triggered by overestimating your learning pace.

The bundle’s value hinges on alignment with systems engineering goals. If the course content maps to building APIs, CLI tools, or DevOps utilities, it acts as a cohesive curriculum, reducing cognitive load. The financial strain mechanism is another critical factor. If the cost becomes a source of stress, it distracts from learning, creating a feedback loop where underutilization amplifies regret. Conversely, if you can commit to the study plan and verify market alignment, the bundle acts as a structured accelerator, potentially yielding a break-even point within 1-2 years via career advancement.

If any of these conditions are unmet, opt for lower-cost alternatives (e.g., “The Go Programming Language” book, open-source contributions). While less efficient, they avoid the financial regret mechanism triggered by underutilization.

In conclusion, the Ardan Labs bundle is a high-stakes investment that functions as a structured accelerator under optimal conditions. However, its failure modes—financial strain, misalignment, and underutilization—are unforgiving. If you cannot meet the commitment threshold or verify alignment, the lower-cost, flexible path is the mechanically superior choice, trading efficiency for financial resilience.

South Korea’s ruling party formally proposes deferral of crypto income tax

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

South Korea's ruling party leader has officially proposed that the government further delay the crypto tax.

Democratic Party leader Kim Min-seok said at a party supreme council meeting at the National Assembly that the decision followed consultations through the Strategy and Finance Committee, government-party talks, and meetings with crypto asset businesses, local news outlet News1 reported on Wednesday.

Kim stated that party members agreed that the country should first enact the Digital Asset Basic Act, a comprehensive legal framework for crypto, and give companies and investors time to adapt to new rules.

The tax plan, currently scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, would impose a 22% tax (including local tax) on annual gains from the transfer or lending of crypto assets above a basic deduction of 2.5 million won ($1,868.7). If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

The ruling party leader acknowledged past deferrals of the tax and said that this time the party would set a clear deferral period while thoroughly preparing prerequisites, including use of overseas transaction information, IT system verification and taxpayer guidance.

Last week, South Korea's presidential office was reportedly discussing postponement of the levy until 2029 to align with the U.S. timeline for joining the OECD's Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework.

Kathy Hochul’s Win in New York Assures a Hollywood Home for Years to Come

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The pro-entertainment governor coasted to victory. Producers (if not California officials) should feel elated.

Outside of California, no state matters more to the entertainment business than New York.

After all, the industry was arguably born in the region — Manhattan is where Thomas Edison held the first public screening of his Kinetoscope and Westchester County was an early hub of silent film and a movie star getaway. In the century since, New York has proven an irreplicably cinegenic backdrop. (Apologies to Sydney and Toronto, but there is simply no substitute.) It’s also a second home to countless celebrities who enjoy the luxury of changing seasons. Related Stories News Pedro Pascal, Olivia Rodrigo, Dave Bautista, Jennifer Lawrence Among A-Listers Urging Fans to Vote: "Get Out There" Midterms 2026 How Jon Ossoff Won Re-Election and Became the Midterms' Main Character in the Process

And of course it’s where so many film and television productions currently shoot — now even more than California.So what kind of leadership will Hollywood East see after Tuesday’s elections? Here’s the breakdown.Governor’s Race — Hochul PrevailsThis one wasn’t exactly a nail-biter, but Hollywood will surely rejoice that the Democratic incumbent, Kathy Hochul, handily fended off her Republican opponent, Nassau County executive Bruce Blakeman, leading by 47 points with about a fifth of the votes tallied. NBC News has called the race. (The silver lining for Blakeman is that the loss will overshadow has previous claim to fame as “guy whose ex-wife married Paul McCartney.”)

Under Hochul’s governorship, New York has made bold efforts to grow its cinematic footprint. It appears to be working: the state drew $1.6 billion in production spend last quarter, bringing it within striking distance of California as the film industry’s preferred shooting location. It’s no surprise that Hochul earned the endorsement of three of the area’s major entertainment unions, namely Actors’ Equity, IATSE and AFM Local 802.

After decades of watching states like Louisiana, Georgia or New Mexico siphon off film investment and concomitant cultural prestige, New York passed a massive tax credit program worth $800 million a year through 2036 (plus $100 million dedicated to maximizing the benefits for indie productions). Meanwhile, new soundstages have cropped up all around the five boroughs to welcome the expected influx of productions.“Governor Hochul understands that film and television production means jobs, small businesses and a thriving creative economy,” Adam Gordon, who co-founded Astoria’s Wildflower Studios with Robert De Niro, told THR. “She has been a real champion for this industry and we’re enthusiastic about what comes next.” Gordon adds that Wildflower, which was completed in 2024, is now 100% occupied. Among recent productions to have filmed there is this month’s Focker-in-Law.

Hochul further ingratiated herself to the industry by signing the nation’s first laws regulating artificial intelligence in media and advertising. To top it off, she reversed outdated, Footloose-ian restrictions on dancing in New York bars — with the support of Kevin Bacon.

Her supporters may want to cut a rug tonight.

N.Y. Districts 13 and 37 — New Kids on the BlocNew York Mayor — and cinematic scion — Zohran Mamdani may not have been on the ballot, but the rising progressive kingmaker can take partial credit for the resounding victories of insurgent, DSA-aligned congressional candidates he championed in their primary challenges against moderate Democratic incumbents, particularly Darializa Avila Chevalier (NY-13) and Claire Valdez (NY-37).Chevalier, who rose to prominence as a leader of the Pro-Palestinian student movement at Columbia University following Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attacks in Israel, has embraced unabashedly radical policies, including the idea of (eventually) abolishing prisons. (Her most inflammatory past statements could be found in her Twitter history, which she deleted ahead of her primary victory in the spring.) As heads to Washington, Chevalier is poised to supplant Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez as the left’s congressional lightning rod, especially since AOC seems eager to cede lightning-rod duties as she moderates some of her positions ahead of the 2028 presidential election.Chevalier and Valdez —who represents a large chunk of Queens — together with other newly enthroned representatives including Melat Kiros (CO-TK) and Adam Hamawy (NJ-TK), are set to form the core of a progressive coalition within the Democratic caucus, which some are calling “the Bloc,” a younger successor to “The Squad,” the AOC-led faction that rose to prominence during the 2018 Blue Wave, during Trump’s first term. The two campaigned with popular streamer Hasan Piker in the all-important primary, as he both supported the candidates and took some victory laps with their wins.

NY – 17 — She Fought the LawlerThe most dramatic development in the New York races today was the glitch — reportedly a programming error — that affected voting machines in northern Westchester and delayed the tabulation of ballots in one of the states most closely watched congressional contests.“No matter the outcome, every vote should be counted, and that is what is going to happen, and it’s going to happen tonight, and we are going to make sure, one way or the other, we have an answer as to the results tonight,” the two-term Republican incumbent, Mike Lawler, told reporters, according to the New York Post. Quickly quashing voting-machine-related conspiracy theories might seem atypical of a party that, following the President’s lead, has sought to sow doubt ahead of the election. But Lawler is among the most moderate of GOP congressmen, representing a Hudson Valley district where registered Democrats outnumber Republicans.His challenger is political newcomer Cait Conley, a retired Army officer who served six tours overseas, and who has likewise staked out moderate positions, realizing NY-17 lies well outside the Mamdani-verse. She has sought mainly to campaign on lowering the cost of living, and to tie her opponent to Trump’s unpopular tariffs and foreign wars.Among the Hollywood figures to have weighed on this race are Carrie Coon (The Gilded Age; The White Lotus), who campaigned for Conley,and Edie Falco(The Sopranos; Nurse Jackie), who endorsed the Democrat — as did former Bill Clinton and Barack Obama aide (and Ari brother) Rahm Emmanuel.

How Oregon State turned a raw athlete into a starting linebacker

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CORVALLIS — Two years ago, Jarrel Comer was a freshman at Tallahassee Community College and had not played competitive football since middle school. Now he's a starting outside linebacker at Oregon State.

Comer played basketball at Eastside High School in Gainesville, Florida, but began college as a regular student. He looked for opportunities to play sports again and football became the opportunity at Citrus College in California.

He boarded a plane for the first time to take a cross-country flight to play football for the first time in more than four years.

"I was willing to take that chance to get to where I am today," Comer said.

He had 58 tackles (8.5 for loss), including six sacks at the California junior college and got on the radar of linebackers coach Cort Dennison, then at Idaho. Dennison kept pursuing Comer at OSU, where he signed in January and arrived during the spring.

Justin Joyner wins first game as Oregon State coach in blowout

Atkinson, Legree join Nike's Blue Ribbon Elite as OSU extends apparel deal

Pac-12 confirms play clock error led to penalty on Oregon State in its loss to Fresno State

At 6-foot-4 and 245 pounds, Comer was dominating in junior college from his athleticism alone. He didn't know the finer points of playing football. Hand placement, setting the edge in the run game and pass coverage were not in his repertoire. Comer was there to pin his ears back and attack the quarterback, regardless of the down and distance.

"They didn't ask much of me," Comer said. "They told me just do what I do, use my athleticism, get to the quarterback."

What Comer lacks in experience, he makes up for in ability. He consistently ranks among OSU's fastest players in practice, which was invaluable to a team seeking more pass rush.

"His athleticism is off the charts," defensive coordinator Mike MacIntyre said. "We see him grow leaps and bounds. Playing a lot more (against Montana), he will grow leaps and bounds going into this Saturday because now he's seen it, it slows down, he understands the different things."

RELATED: Experts release Oregon State vs. UTEP predictions, best bets, and preview for Week 4

Comer has 10 tackles thus far, including four in each of the past two games, and is already more than a situational pass rusher for the Beavers. He credits MacIntyre and edge coaches Joe Seumalo and George Frazier with helping him learn the game, particularly pass coverage.

Ideally, pairing Comer with Takari Hickle will allow OSU to generate more pressure as teams won't be able to focus all their offensive line on one side.

"Teams have to respect his speed," Hickle said. "If they don't, then he'll run right by them. He is definitely a threat in coverage and in the pass game."

TransUnion: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cloud platform migrations are cutting legacy costs while accelerating new product launches across the business.

On its third-quarter earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, TransUnion (TRU -0.86%) highlighted broad-based revenue growth driven by international markets and core product diversification. Broader vertical expansion and technology modernization help shelter the credit bureau from cyclical swings in U.S. mortgage lending and rising interest rates. Three things management said stand out.

Insurance has evolved into a key growth pillar, with non-credit solutions such as driver history and fraud prevention providing steady revenue regardless of underlying economic cycles.

Concerns over the Federal Housing Finance Agency (FHFA), the regulator overseeing housing lenders, switching from tri-merge credit reports that combine three bureaus to bi-merge reports that combine two are manageable because most mortgage revenue rests outside mandated requirements.

Moving core data operations to OneTru, the company's single global cloud platform, reduces legacy computing costs while enabling teams to build and deploy new analytical products faster.

International expansion and software innovation continue to power financial performance, giving management room to return cash to shareholders through buybacks and dividends. Low leverage ratios, high free cash flow conversion, and automated platform tools give the business durable pricing power across economic cycles.

Management is looking past near-term rate headwinds by cementing market share in emerging verticals and expanding digital identity offerings across global regions.

Upcoming Paramount+ Shows Reportedly Eying Move To HBO Max

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The future of Paramount+ shows is getting clearer.

With Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, one of the biggest questions was what would happen to the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services. The post-merger company, which is now known as Skydance, appointed HBO's Casey Bloys as the Co-Chair and Chief Content Officer of Skydance Direct-to-Consumer, in which he is responsible for overseeing both Paramount+ and HBO Max, and co-CEO David Ellison has discussed plan to eventually merge the two services.

A new report from Deadline now shares that while no official decisions have been made yet, the possibility is being discussed of moving Paramount+'s upcoming drama series to HBO Max. Potential candidates include Nicole Kidman and Elle Fanning's legal thriller Discretion, the 9/11 first responders show The Best of Us led by Succession's Jeremy Strong, and other projects that are in the midst of waiting for an official greenlight.

Conversations about this move have reportedly reached the highest levels of Skydance, but all Paramount+ shows are staying on the platform for now as talks continue to progress. Meanwhile, Francesca Orsi, who was promoted from EVP to President of HBO Drama Series and Films, is working with Bloys to decide which drama projects belong on HBO.

As part of reviewing the Paramount+ slate with Sarah Aubrey, Head of Paramount+, Max Originals and International Programming, Orsi allegedly may also be tasked with looking at which Paramount+ series feel aligned with HBO, along with working on the select shows that stay on Paramount+, especially if they are akin to HBO's prestige dramas.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

While the new streaming structure is still being worked out, moving upcoming Paramount+ shows like Discretion and The Best of Us to HBO Max could potentially expedite plans to merge the platforms together. From Kidman's time on Big Little Lies and The Undoing and Strong playing Kendall Roy in Succession, the stars of these series have plenty of Emmy Award-winning experience with HBO, making their new prestige dramas a natural fit.

This may be the trajectory for Paramount+'s upcoming shows, but it does not answer what will happen to the ones that already exist. From Landman to The Madison, there is no shortage of ongoing Taylor Sheridan shows on Paramount+, but it is unclear whether they will also move to HBO Max, especially before his NBCUniversal television deal begins in 2029. There's also the matter of where unscripted shows will go, including the many reality ones that also air on CBS, such as Big Brother, Survivor, and The Amazing Race.

If all Paramount+ content eventually shifts to HBO Max, there is additionally the matter of whether Skydance's streaming service will just be called HBO Max, if it will combine names, or if there will be a complete rebranding. Before the merger, HBO's streaming platform already underwent many name changes, including HBO Go, HBO Now, HBO Max, Max, and eventually back to HBO Max. Another major and more pressing question is what the subscription prices will look like if the two services and all their movies and shows are fully merged.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.
