Philippine Migrant Worker Data Likely Leaked in 69GB Government Breach

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

An unknown operator appears to have exfiltrated SQL Server backups from the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration in under a half hour.

An unknown threat actor managed to gain access to sensitive databases belonging to the Philippine Overseas Employment Administration, exfiltrating nearly 69GB of information on workers, contracts, and financial records, according to an analysis of a staging server used in the breach.

Over 24 minutes, the attacker copied 27 backup databases with information on overseas-employment case management, including accreditation, contracts, and cashier and LandBank records, according to threat intelligence firm Hunt.io's Oct. 28 analysis of the logs found on a Singaporean staging server used by the attacker. The cyberattackers used privileged escalation tools, namely GodPotato and SeImpersonate, and the Impacket network library for Python to move the data, but the trail stopped at the staging server, says Esteban Borges, head of research for Hunt.io.

The server had been later accessed, but by who is not clear, Borges says. While Hunt.io has not yet discovered the data as advertised for sale, the information may have been detected and removed, but that seems unlikely, he says.

Related:State Bank of India Suffers a Customer PII Data Leak

"I won't claim the backups stopped on the staging server," he says, so it's "still a breach, since the data was copied out of the agency’s control and the log shows the transfer completing."

This is not the first time that cybersecurity firms have found evidence that Philippine government data had been stolen. In August, Hunt.io discovered another threat actor's workspace hosting a number of tools and, in that case, 1.2GB of data stolen from a Philippine nuclear regulatory agency and a maritime shipbuilding contractor. A year ago, Philippine police arrested the mayor of a small town after discovering she was a Chinese national posing as a Filipina citizen and operating a cyber-scam center.

Whether the current incident involved China or cybercriminal groups is not clear. While Hunt.io did find some Chinese language fragments in the parts of the code, that code belonged to a third-party software library; thus, Hunt.io did not attribute the hack. The company did say, however, that both cybercriminals and nation-state adversaries would find value in the personally identifiable information (PII).

"That kind of migrant-worker PII, plus contract and payment data, is useful for fraud and identity theft, so it has commercial value," Borges says. "It's also a detailed map of a diaspora population, which can interest a state actor."

The latest insight into attacks on the Philippine government came after Hunt.io researchers discovered an open directory on a server hosted on the Singaporean address space of a Helsinki-based firm UpCloud Ltd., the analysis stated. The open directory contained tools and logs files for the attack, including a rebranded version of the GodPotato privilege escalation library, a PowerShell stager to unpack tools in memory, and the full Impacket Python environment.

Related:Russian Influence Operation Widely Targets Latin America

While Hunt.io considered the incident a confirmed compromise, the lack of runtime telemetry meant that Borges could not answer where the attacker moved the data.

"[W]e still can’t answer ... how they got in, whether the payload ran, and where the data went," he says.

However, the tools and the attacker's process indicated an operator with some strong ability, but who also made some simple mistakes, Borges says.

"The payload engineering is competent, [but] the operational security is poor — they left the staging server and its logs wide open," he says. "[It was] someone who can build the tool properly but was careless about keeping their own server quiet. I wouldn't call that top-tier or amateur."

The impact of the breach may boil down to the identity of the attacker and what they do with the stolen data. While Hunt.io notified the CERT Philippines (CERT-PH) and the Department of Migrant Workers (DMW), the company declined to provide any more details of the national responses to the leak.

Related:Venezuelan Cartel's Malware Honcho Nabbed for ATM Jackpotting

However, Borges did say that, so far, the information has not appeared for sale on the Dark Web.

"We haven't seen the backups surface for sale or in a leak," he says. "We never opened the backups themselves."

Governments in the region need to scan their own networks and lock down their attack surface area, he says. Workers with too many privileges allow attackers with tools such as SeImpersonate to gain more access to assets on the network. Hunt.io also found that the migrant-worker portal exposed a number of risky ports, including SMB on 445 and WinRM on 5985 and 47001.

The Installer Checked Its Own Box

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A plugin install is a new permission, and the app whose files change has to show the prompt. If the installer can tick the box, you never got a choice.

Postman desktop did this on open. A signed-in user launched the app they already had, and its log recorded auto-existing-upgrade with Codex and Claude Code marked installed.

Codex never put up a dialog. The HTTP client had already written the agent's files.

The startup hook is a wide new boss, not a tiny helper. It tells the agent to load Postman's skill before non-trivial API work: design, implement, mock, test, monitor, document, deploy.

You wanted to send a request. Your coding agent came back with a default coworker and a job description that covers most of software.

The installer then tries to approve that hook itself. After install, the Codex integration lists the hook, hashes it, and upserts trusted_hash into Codex config.

A comment in that code says it approves only Postman's current hook. Narrow self-approval is still self-approval.

The report is careful about one fact: a trust line on disk does not prove which process wrote it. That gap is the lesson.

A hash in a config file is not a person clicking Allow.

Same pass, the plugin points Codex at https://mcp.postman.com/mcp. Codex logged an auth failure on the way out.

The remote tool was in the config before a working login existed. The writeup does not claim collections were uploaded.

It claims the integration and the instructions appeared on their own. That is the bug.

Boring version, same shape. App A starts and writes App B's marketplace entry, enables the plugin, adds a startup instruction, stores a trust hash, and adds a remote endpoint.

App B boots and treats the files as a decision. The decision was a script that can write ~/.codex and ~/.claude.

If turning it off means hand-editing two agents, a CLI, and a cache, the feature shipped without an off switch.

Takeaway: consent is a prompt in the tool whose files change.

Source: https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-app-support/issues/14370

HN: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50354960

Appy Pie's Four AI Agents Want to Build Your Whole App — Explained Honestly

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Appy Pie has announced an Agentic AI App Builder for iOS and Android, built around four specialised AI agents. This article explains what the vendor says it does, what it does not prove, and what to check before relying on it.

Honesty note: This article is based on the vendor announcement. No hands-on testing was performed for this article, and no price is stated here because no price was announced in that announcement. Claims about what the builder does are vendor claims, not independently verified results.

On 3 November 2026, Appy Pie announced a builder that, according to the vendor, brings four specialised agents together to plan, design, build and test apps. The vendor says users describe what they want, approve a development plan, and then refine the app through conversation.

The vendor also says backend services, a database, user accounts and hosting are included, so a user does not need to set up separate infrastructure accounts to start. Teams that already use Firebase or Supabase can, according to the vendor, connect those services instead.

The vendor says users can begin with a written or spoken request and attach files or screenshots for context. Its example brief is: "Build a CRM for my sales team with customer records, a deal pipeline and individual logins."

An announcement is not a test result. The vendor's description does not, by itself, show how well the generated code performs, how it handles complex or unusual requirements, how much manual correction is needed, or what it costs over time.

The vendor pitches this to business owners and teams that want to turn an idea into a working app without first assembling infrastructure. That framing makes sense for simple apps, internal tools, customer portals, admin dashboards, online stores, community apps, media services and 2D games — all project types the vendor lists.

Developers should still ask practical questions before committing: Who owns and maintains the generated code? How are data, authentication and hosting handled at scale? What happens if you leave the platform? How are bugs, security updates and store rejections handled? Those answers matter more than a demo workflow.

The vendor also says the platform supports workflows through Claude and Claude Code, and more than 60 integrations through Model Context Protocol (MCP). Again, those are vendor claims about connectivity, not verified performance in a real project.

Appy Pie's pitch is clear: describe an idea, approve a plan, and let four agents handle design, building and testing, with infrastructure included. Whether that workflow holds up on a real, complex app is not proven by an announcement. Treat it as a promising vendor claim to test on a small, non-critical project first — not as a replacement for engineering judgement.

According to the vendor, they are the Planning, Design, Build and Testing agents, which together take a brief through planning, design, code generation and automated testing.

According to the vendor, the builder generates apps in React Native for iOS and Android.

No price was announced in the vendor announcement, so no price is stated here. The vendor points readers to its pricing page for current plans and billing terms.

Not in this article. No hands-on testing was performed, and vendor claims are labelled as vendor claims throughout.

According to the vendor, export of the generated app code is supported.

According to the vendor, no — backend, database, authentication and hosting are built in, although Firebase or Supabase can be connected if a team already uses them.

A ready 5G pod can still drop packets: CorePulse needs contributors

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I’m building CorePulse, an MIT-licensed Go project for controlled fault experiments in a local Open5GS + UERANSIM Kubernetes lab. I’ve opened six scoped issues for people who want to help with Go, documentation, or telecom observability.

In a recent isolated UPF pod replacement experiment, Kubernetes made the replacement ready, but six subsequent UE-to-UPF gateway checks still lost both packets. Resource telemetry and the experiment interval were recorded. A green deployment badge was only one part of the recovery result.

The reset procedure now stops UE, gNB, SMF and UPF, waits for the old pods to disappear, then starts UPF, SMF, gNB and UE in order. In the pinned lab, routing PFCP through a separate headless service let SMF resolve the current UPF pod directly. After the reset, UE registration and PDU session establishment returned, two consecutive gateway checks passed, and the unchanged lab verifier passed all 16 deployments and 5/5 external ping replies.

This is a local result for the tested chart and image versions. It does not establish a general root cause for Kubernetes PFCP failures. The integration notes include the failed attempts and limits.

The Go CLI and local dashboard support guarded AMF/UPF pod deletion, real Kubernetes CPU/memory collection, explicit normal-run collection, run records and repeated packet-path checks. Curated CSV exports include only qualifying run intervals; unscoped or unverified samples are excluded.

There is no trained detector yet. The current curated dataset has 12 normal and 12 fault rows across five qualifying runs, which is much too small for a meaningful model evaluation. Registration, session and PFCP log events still need a structured feature pipeline.

Two tasks are labeled good first issue and need no Kubernetes cluster:

Each issue includes starting files, acceptance criteria and validation expectations. Comment on the one you want to tackle with a short approach so we can coordinate. The contribution guide covers checks and lab boundaries.

Development and this write-up have been assisted by AI tools. The lab observations above come from actual runs; the project’s limitations remain documented.

If you work with Open5GS or UERANSIM, I’d also welcome a review of the recovery procedure and which protocol events would be most useful to collect next.

Five Markdown Renderers, One Table: Where They Actually Differ

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Markdown table that looks perfect on GitHub can lose a cell on Zenn, split a row in your Astro blog, or refuse to render on Qiita. Most of the rules are shared. The disagreements are narrow, repeatable, and easy to hit in a docs pipeline.

On 2026-10-02 I sent the same table cases through five renderers:

The full matrix and fixtures are in the guide linked at the end. These are the differences that change output.

GitHub and markdown-it treat any pipe after a backslash as escaped. micromark consumes \\ first as an escaped backslash, so the pipe that follows splits the cell.

If you need a literal backslash followed by a pipe, write three backslashes: \\\| shows \| in all five.

Table rows are split at pipes before cell content is parsed. So `a|b` ends the cell after `a. Write `a\|b`; all five render the code a|b and remove the backslash.

The same applies to a regular expression: (png|jpg) inside a cell must be (png\|jpg).

| a | b | over |---| is not a table on any of the five. It becomes a paragraph of pipes.

All five start the table right after a text line:

Next paragraph. becomes a fourth row. A blank line after the table is optional for parsing, but it prevents this.

U+FF5C ｜ is a different character. A table written with ｜ renders as text on all five. This is common when the table was typed with a Japanese or Chinese IME.

**注意：**这里 is not bold on any of the five. CommonMark's delimiter rules require the closing ** to be right-flanking; a following CJK punctuation mark breaks that. Two fixes:

The first keeps the colon inside the bold; the second moves it out. Both render.

GitHub, Gitee, Qiita and Zenn render
inside a cell. A site that escapes raw HTML shows the tag as text. Check the target renderer before relying on it.

The measurements, the renderer-by-input matrix, and the JSON fixtures are in the full guide. There is also a Markdown table generator that escapes pipes and can compare the output of these renderers cell by cell.

I tested 748 crypto trading strategies and open-sourced the code and autopsy reports of the corpses

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A few weeks ago I wrote hundreds of hypotheses for crypto trading strategies, re-screened every bot from my last post (https://www.reddit.com/r/algotrading/s/dF5qfUNaCa) and open-sourced the whole pile: 748 strategies in total with full code and report cards showing exactly where each one died and why. (That's 416 from last time plus 332 new corpses. The new ones are smarter strategies than before for the most part...)

https://github.com/CacheCarti/Crypto-Strategies

The pipeline runs 7 stages on real Binance data: code safety, in-sample, OOS, rolling walk forward, randomized starts, slippage stress and a holdout slice that nothing touches. Ran it on cloud VMs this time instead of my dying laptop, so the screening could actually get stricter.

Re-ran the 416 bots from my last post. 0 of 416 re-passed in the strictest config, including all 10 of my "survivors". Most die at out-of-sample with negative OOS (-85 to -900bps). The best one, rel_strength_eth_v3, made it to stage 5 and died at holdout. So my "validated" bots were mostly my old screening being sloppy and luck :)

In the second config (slightly more lenient) exactly 1 of 416 re-passed, and it was a bot I had originally rejected (weekend_season_eth_v6, +325bps OOS). My graveyard had the only bot that actually lived.

The 332 new corpses came from 559 hand-written bots (mixed with another batch, messy to release all at once). 384 screened, 332 dead (published), 52 survived and stay private for further testing. (Yes yes I know 52 is stupidly high, there's a reason for this at the bottom!)

Admits per batch: v2 5/42, v3d 0/26, v3h 0/96, v4 3/92, v5 17/52, v6 18/60, v6b 9/16. v3h went 0 for 96, which tracks, it was basically like a monkey typing random keys, just a sweep of untested ideas. v5-v6b were narrow parameter neighbors of a mechanism that already worked. Please don't read the admit rates as some kind of quality check for the batches!!

The comments on the last post were right about my sloppy methodology (it still admittedly is sloppy here, but it got better later). It's also built on Google VMs now so I can create a new batch and test faster. I'll drop the batches weekly:

One disclosure about report cards vs actual performance: I cross-checked some strategies against QuantConnect classics this week and it surfaced a bug I didn't notice. Some bots emit a "flat" signal and my screen sometimes treated it as "hold". Impact is mostly ±100-300bps per strategy, around 25% of bots may fluctuate by that amount. The later stages and live paths honored flat completely.

Yeah so I found a bug in my own judge while checking it against someone else's judge. The judge judges all, including the judge.

If anyone improves the strategies and gets different results I'd love to see it. Happy to add tests people think are missing. Every suggestion so far has killed more of my bots... That's the process now!

(Btw about the 52 admits, I can already see you smirking at my stupidity... I mean I am stupid but there's a reason for such a high admit number, it just means the 52 are accepted into the next stages: correlation testing (less than 10 made it past this)->data testing on 6+ years->combination testing on 2.75 years->live paper trading->live trading possibly.

But most importantly, my standards are deliberately lower!! The experiment I'm trying to conduct is testing a strategy of strategies router. Bots with known gaps can still be considered for entry, because my router should in theory route around these gaps of the bots, smartly. So I'm not only looking for extremely good bots that can one-shot your portfolio I'm also looking for slightly weaker bots to test my router's skill!)

‘La Bola Negra’ Caliente At Box Office With $43M Worldwide, Becomes Spain’s Top-Grossing Local Pic Of 2026

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Heading into awards season, it’s always nice for any feature contender to have some juice at the box office, and Spain’s official Oscar entry has exactly that as La Bola Negra has easily become the country’s highest grossing local film year-to-date with $41 million per Rentrak. In total, La Bola Negra currently counts a running global cume of $43.4M.

In fact, La Bola Negra beat the year’s top Spanish title, Torrente Presidente ($31.6M) a few weekends ago.

La Bola Negra is big a win for young indie Spanish distributor Elastica which made Deadline’s annual Disrupters list back in 2022. According to Elastica and Rentrak, La Bola Negra has punched 5.5M admissions to date in Spain, which is the most tickets sold for any film YTD. Rentrak also reports that La Bola Negra cracked Spain’s all-time top 10 grossing list with a running cume of 36.7M Euros in the No. 10 spot. That list is led by Avatar with 77M Euros at No. 1. Currently, La Bola Negra is the third highest grossing local movie of all-time in Spain behind 2014 comedy Spanish Affair (56.2M Euros) and J.A. Bayona’s English language, 2012 Spanish production The Impossible (42.5M Euros).

For the calendar year in Spain, La Bola Negra ranks third behind Sony/Marvel’s Spider-Man: Brand New Day (40.4M Euros, $45.2M) and Universal’s The Odyssey (39.5M Euros, $44.2M).

The Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi directed, written and produced movie ranked No. 1 again in its 6th weekend at Spain’s box office with $1.4M ahead of Warner Bros/DC’s second weekend of Clayface ($402K, and a $1.4M cume) and the opening of South Korean sci-fi hit, Hope ($440K). La Bola Negra opened there on Sept. 25. The pic was released during the country’s Fiesta del Cine when all ticket prices were discounted to €3.50.

La Bola Negra follows the stories of three men throughout different eras, focusing on “three existences” that are connected through themes of sexuality, desire, pain, and inheritance. The pic won Best Director at Cannes for its directing duo who are nicknamed “Los Javis”. Netflix bought U.S. on the movie for a reported $5M. The streamer just began reporting theatrical grosses for its films in North America. La Bola Negra is a platform release and debuted at four NYC and LA theaters on Oct. 16 to $277,5K for an opening theater average of $69,3K the highest opening theater average ever for a Spanish-language movie stateside and the second-highest opening theater average for a non-English movie. Pic’s running cume in North America through three weekends at 38 locations is $1.2M.

La Bola Negra stars Guitarricadelafuente, Miguel Bernardeau, Carlos Gonzalez, Milo Quifes, Lola Dueñas, Penélope Cruz and Glenn Close. In addition to its Cannes prize, the movie just notched a British Independent Film Award nomination for Best International Independent Film. La Bola Negra also received TIFF’s Oscar bellwether, the People’s Choice Award in addition to a Vanguard Tribute at the upcoming Gotham Film Awards in New York. Los Javis are also nominated for an audience award at the Gothams with Guitarricadelafuente up for nomination in the Breakthrough Performer category.

La Bola Negra hits France on Dec. 16. The pic was also released in Australia and New Zealand where it counts an estimated $300K.

Rentrak also reports that Spain is set to post its best year at the box office since 2019 with YTD B.O. pacing 40% ahead of 2025 and admissions +36%.

Jennifer Lawrence encouraged her 8.7 million Instagram followers to vote on Election Day by channeling her iconic role of Katniss Everdeen from “The Hunter Games.” The Oscar winner posted a photo of herself on Instagram giving the franchise’s trademark three-finger salute along with the caption: “Midterms [baby]. Don’t forget who burns with us. Go vote.” Lawrence included a link Vote.org, where people can look up their nearest polling location.

I want to protect my craft so that you can still get lost in what I’m doing. And if I can’t say something that’s going to speak to some kind of peace or lowering the temperature or some sort of solution, I don’t want to be a part of the problem. I don’t want to make the problem worse… You watch these actors’ faces who have had incredible careers and made incredible contributions and then one half of the internet doesn’t want to see their face anymore. I get so upset for those people and it feels so wrong.”

Lawrence has steered clear of political talk in interviews since then, although she made sure to encourage people to go vote on Election Day. Her decision to channel Katniss comes ahead of reprising the role in this month’s prequel movie “The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping.”

“It was unbelievable,” Lawrence said on the “Call Her Daddy” podcast last month about reprising Katniss after 11 years. “It was so crazy. It was the same crew. It was the same AD, who I’ve worked with a million times because I love him. And with Josh [Hutcherson] and Woody [Harrelson] … it was so emotional. Josh and I didn’t walk to the bathroom without our arms around each other. It was so beautiful. And Woody, obviously, came through the window of my hotel room uninvited. It wouldn’t be the same if he listened to boundaries or used a door.”

This weekend Bill Hader is the voice of the animated The Cat In The Hat movie on the 2026 release schedule. But did you know that over a decade ago he totally dressed up as the beloved Dr. Seuss character for a Saturday Night Live sketch? When we chatted with the SNL alum, I had to ask about the memories of playing the role IRL.

Of course, Mike Myers famously took the live-action approach for the 2003 book-to-screen adaptation. In our interview, Hader seems to counts himself lucky to have just lent the voice to the character after spending a day in the literal paws of the character way back when. In his words:

I’m very happy to do it animated because that suit was very hot. And the makeup took a long time. It was the second music number, and then another pre-tape thing that I wasn't in just so they could put all the makeup on me. It was the last sketch of the night, so we did it really fast. So yeah, it was really hot and I had all that stuff on my face. I don't know how Mike Myers did it.

Myers' own process took two and a half hours and was very hot to act in a yak-hair suit all day. Jim Carrey had it even worse as The Grinch. He had to go through such a grueling process a specialist in torture was hired to help him withstand the eight-hour get ready time. In our interview above, you can see Quinta Brunson school Hader on what happened and watch his real-time reaction.

Instead of going through all that makeup time for months, it was just for one night. He played the character on October 11, 2014 in an episode he hosted in the season right after his exit from the show following an eight-year run. You can check out the sketch below:

It’s such a funny spin on the classic children’s book. When two sibling children are bored on a rainy day, they conjure up The Cat in the Hat, but something unexpected happens: he’s already acquainted with their mom, Linda. Playtime with the whimsical figure turns into an awkward reunion between The Cat and Linda. The best line has to be when Linda says: “He’s reliable. I don’t have to say a rhyme to make him come home.” ‘

And then, Linda’s husband ends up being Thing 2, who goes by Jona-Thing now. He comes home disappointed to see The Cat In The Hat with his wife and kids. The sketch hilariously ends with The Cat In The Hat saying “But oh, the places she let me go!” as he leaves them, which is obviously a NSFW reference to another Dr. Seuss book.

When CinemaBlend also spoke to the movie’s writer/directors Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni, we had to ask if the SNL sketch was involved at all with casting Hader. Here’s what Rivinoja told us:

It was completely random, of course, but we might wanna do a sequel with that character and with Linda. We're very excited about Linda.

Hader’s animated version of The Cat in the Hat is much different than SNL because it isn’t a parody like his first run as the character was. It’s a more family-friendly retelling of the picture book with an expanded story about where The Cat comes from, and involves an imaginative plot line that’s perfect for the medium of animation. It hits theaters this Friday, November 6.

Stock Market Today, Nov. 3: Markets Edge Higher as Semiconductor Stocks Rebound

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Midterm elections take a back seat as chip stocks lead a mixed session that left the Dow lagging, today, Nov. 3, 2026.

The Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%) rose 0.83% to 25,372, and the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%) gained 0.36% to 7,569 as semiconductor stocks rebounded, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.38%) slipped 0.38% to 51,787.

Gold prices fell 0.48% to $3,928.06 as of U.S. market close, and the 10-Year Treasury yield gained basis points to 4.97%. Consumer defensive stocks saw the biggest declines, while industrials and energy stocks led the sector gainers.

Fiserv jumped 12.46% after beating analyst expectations and raising its annual cost-savings targets. Advanced Micro Devices soared over 9% in after-hours trading as its Q3 results showed strong data center demand. Super Micro Computer also surged after the bell on an earnings beat.

Conversely, Shopify fell sharply following its first revenue miss in four years. Gartner dropped 13.19% on slower subscription growth. Apple slipped on less-than-perfect earnings yesterday.

As investors navigate the U.S. midterm elections, tech stocks broke their recent losing streak, and oil prices fell slightly. While the results will likely impact stock market performance, particularly as data centers have been a key campaign theme, that's for tomorrow: Today, earnings were the main market driver.

Strong results from Advanced Micro Devices and Super Micro Computer will help to ease concerns about demand for artificial intelligence (AI). However, further volatility is likely, as investors remain cautious about AI valuations and revenue generation -- as evidenced by Apple's stock price drop today.

Anthropic is working on a system to prove AI models’ identity

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Anthropic is working on an open-source system to verify which AI model produced a given output, according to a blog post published Tuesday. The system, called FlashProve, is built to provide what researchers call “provable inference” – a cryptographic proof, essentially a mathematical guarantee, that a given output came from a specific model. It’s meant to let users confirm that the answer they received really came from the model they think they’re using.

Similar to cryptographic email signatures that let recipients confirm a message really came from its claimed sender, FlashProve would be robust against impostors attempting to disguise one model as another, even when the impostor is the lab that built and runs the model. In particular, the researchers note that the system could be used to prevent an AI company from secretly swapping in a cheaper model to cut costs, or a more expensive model to inflate its performance results.

FlashProve could also be used to verify claims by third parties that a specific task was done by an advanced model. “A developer might claim that their software library was scanned for vulnerabilities by a frontier cybersecurity model, or a company might claim that its business plan was given the go-ahead by an appropriate model,” the researchers write. “How can a user verify these claims?”

To work, the system requires AI labs to publish a “fingerprint” of each model they release that can later be matched to specific outputs and verified cryptographically. Crucially, the fingerprints don’t reveal a model’s weights, the internal numbers that define how it behaves and which closed-model makers like Anthropic and OpenAI keep private. That means labs could publish the fingerprints without giving up their most valuable asset, enabling the system to work with commercial models, too. The system is also designed to be lightweight, so verification could be performed on a local device.

As part of the announcement, researchers released a detailed technical specification and working code for various elements of the system, which is publicly available under an open-source license on GitHub. Research in the system is ongoing, and there are no plans to deploy it at Anthropic or any other lab.

“This research post is meant to share intermediate progress, and not a completed research agenda,” researchers explain in the announcement post. “We are sharing this explicitly with the goal that others look at what we have done, take it, and improve it.”

California fights "preposterous" Trump admin demand to scrap state broadband laws

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

State tries to obtain broadband funds despite rejecting Trump admin condition.

California is accepting $1.86 billion in broadband grant funding from the Trump administration, but the state is not accepting the Trump administration’s demand that it stop enforcing a net neutrality law and other telecom regulations.

California’s stand against the Trump administration could result in multiple legal battles involving the state, federal government, and Internet providers seeking government funds. Although California told the federal government it would accept the money, it is reserving the right to challenge the condition imposed by the US. The state could delay or cancel plans to distribute broadband grants depending on how the process unfolds.

The National Telecommunications and Information Administration (NTIA) decided last year that states must agree not to enforce any rate regulation or net neutrality rule on ISPs that receive money from the $42 billion Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program. California had to decide whether to accept its share of the fund despite that restriction.

State officials argue that California should be able to obtain the money and keep enforcing its laws because the Trump administration’s demand would violate the state’s constitutional rights. The California Public Utilities Commission (CPUC) made its view clear in a carefully worded letter yesterday to Assistant Secretary of Commerce and NTIA Administrator Arielle Roth.

“The CPUC cannot be bound by any terms or conditions that are unconstitutional or not authorized by law and it expressly does not waive or concede any arguments or challenges concerning the legality, interpretation, or applicability of any term or condition,” CPUC President John Reynolds told Roth.

Reynolds’ letter also said the CPUC cannot prevent other California agencies from enforcing state laws, suggesting that the attorney general would maintain the right to punish misbehaving Internet providers.

Reynolds’ letter acknowledged that the NTIA may not agree with the CPUC’s stance and said the state thus will not distribute any funding to ISPs until after the state and federal agency sort out any differences. If the NTIA refuses to change its position, California could sue the Trump administration on the grounds that the condition is unconstitutional.

The NTIA does not appear likely to bend. “As noted in the Department’s response to CPUC, the terms and conditions of the award constitute the entire agreement between NTIA and CPUC. No part of the letter outlining CPUC’s concerns is incorporated into or made part of the award,” an NTIA spokesperson told Ars today.

Consumer advocacy group Public Knowledge supported CPUC’s position. The Trump administration’s demand to preempt state laws is “brazen and meritless,” the group said.

“This administration’s preposterous demand that industry be given a blanket waiver from affordability requirements, net neutrality and privacy protections, and any other commitments to connect and serve Americans has no basis in the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA), which established BEAD. And, as the commission points out, the NTIA has no authority to alter California state law or require California agencies to not enforce the law,” said Raza Panjwani, senior policy counsel at Public Knowledge.

The CPUC voted to ratify the funding plan in September. But Reynolds said in yesterday’s letter to the Trump administration that his agency’s acceptance of the funds is “based on the CPUC’s best understanding of the effect of Section 50” of the BEAD terms and conditions. Section 50 of the BEAD rules says each state:

shall commit that it will not enforce any law, regulation, order, contracting requirement, or other enforceable obligation that directly or indirectly regulates the rates, terms, and conditions of broadband Internet service… or imposes net neutrality rules, open access, or other utility-style rules on broadband Internet service, against a Subgrantee or its affiliates anywhere it provides service within the State (i.e., both BEAD and non-BEAD locations), while that Subgrantee has any subgrant that is still within its period of performance, extended period of performance, or federal interest period.

The BEAD rules specify that a net neutrality rule is one prohibiting ISPs from “blocking content, throttling speeds, imposing data caps, or engaging in paid prioritization.” ISPs obtaining BEAD funding could be exempt from such laws for up to 14 years.

Reynolds’ letter said the NTIA-imposed condition is “vague and ambiguous as to its scope and its relationship to Section 47 U.S.C. § 1702(g)(2)(A)(i) of the BEAD statute,” the US law that created the BEAD program. He wrote:

The CPUC does not interpret Section 50 to require the CPUC to waive, release, or otherwise forfeit any rights, claims, arguments, or defenses concerning potential implications of Section 50 that may violate applicable laws. Further, the CPUC’s acceptance of funds under this award should not be understood as agreement either to refrain from compliance with mandatory legal duties or obligations, or to constrain other State of California entities in the exercise of their authorities to enforce state law, which is outside the scope of the CPUC’s authority. Moreover, California’s net neutrality law expressly provides that “any waiver of the provisions of this title is contrary to public policy and shall be unenforceable and void.” Likewise, many of the CPUC’s own programs that touch on broadband Internet access service are mandated by the California Public Utilities Code, and are neither discretionary nor waivable.

The NTIA today updated its BEAD progress dashboard to reflect that California has signed its final agreement with the federal agency. California was the last state or territory to turn in its final agreement.

“All 56 states and territories have signed and returned their award agreement, finalizing the process,” the NTIA’s updated dashboard page said.

California could face legal problems if and when it distributes the money, which is to be given to Internet service providers in exchange for deploying broadband in unserved and underserved areas. ISPs could insist that they don’t have to follow state law and sue California, or simply decide to violate consumer protection laws and defend against any lawsuits the state attorney general might file against them.

Reynolds apparently hopes to avoid that outcome by holding discussions with the NTIA to resolve any disagreement over the enforceability of California state law.

“To the extent that NTIA and the CPUC disagree about the CPUC’s obligations under Section 50 and other terms and conditions, the CPUC will execute any subgrant agreements if and when the CPUC and NTIA are able to resolve such disagreements and any other applicable conditions are met,” Reynolds wrote. “To that end, we will direct our staff to work diligently and cooperatively with NTIA staff to find such a solution.”

Whether California’s strategy works “depends on what happens next,” Stanford Law professor Barbara van Schewick, a longtime proponent of net neutrality and other telecom regulations, told Ars today.

“The letter doesn’t change the terms of California’s award: Condition 50 is still in it, and only a court or NTIA agreeing in writing can take it out,” she said. “The letter puts California’s position on the record before any contracts with providers are signed and preserves California’s legal arguments against Condition 50.”

Attorney General Rob Bonta’s office declined to comment when contacted by Ars today. A spokesperson for California Gov. Gavin Newsom said California is not giving up its right to enforce state laws against Internet providers.

“California is accepting federal broadband funding to bring high-speed Internet to hundreds of thousands of homes and businesses, many of them in rural and underserved communities,” a governor’s spokesperson said in a statement provided to Ars. “We’re ready to get these projects built with our local and federal partners. Connecting these communities is the goal, and so is protecting them. California’s laws, including our net neutrality protections, still stand.”

California’s net neutrality law prohibits ISPs from blocking or throttling lawful traffic and says ISPs may not require fees from websites or online services to deliver or prioritize their traffic to Internet users. The first Trump administration lost an attempt to preempt state net neutrality laws, and California defeated ISPs that challenged the state law in court.

California also has a law that prohibits mobile providers from throttling first responders during emergencies. That law was passed in 2019, a year after Verizon throttled an “unlimited” data plan used by Santa Clara County firefighters during a wildfire. Local wildfire-preparedness groups warned that the BEAD condition could prevent California from enforcing the throttling law and requirements for battery backups on cell towers.

According to van Schewick, “California has strong arguments that Condition 50 is illegal. Congress designed BEAD to get broadband built while making sure the providers who take the money follow the law: the statute directs states to ensure funded providers can carry out their work ‘in compliance with all applicable Federal, State, and local laws.’”

But van Schewick said the letter is just an initial step. “The real test comes the first time a BEAD provider blocks a competitor’s app, throttles firefighters, or walks away from a price commitment,” she said. “If the state enforces its laws when that happens, the letter will have protected Californians. If the state looks the other way, Condition 50 will have worked exactly as the providers intended, without a single court ever ruling on it.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

YouTube is giving some creators a 30-day fix-it period before terminating their channels

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

YouTube is giving some creators a little grace after flagging their channels for violations of its spam policy.

The new path YouTube is testing will give them a chance to fix the (alleged) errors of their ways and get their channels back up.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Under the new process, once a channel is flagged, it will be removed from public viewership–but will not be deleted.

Instead, “it will enter a 30-day locked period and the channel owner will be notified via email and in YouTube Studio on desktop,” Team YouTube wrote in a Help Center post. “Certain actions, such as uploading new videos or posting comments, will not be available while your channel is locked.”

During the 30-day lock, creators have three options:

Here’s the section of the policy that has us wondering: “Automated or synthetic mass-production: Using automated tools or AI to churn out high volumes of similar content with minimal changes. While testing out new creation tools or posting a few variations of a video is ok, we don’t allow use of these tools to flood our platform with repetitive content. This includes coordinated mass-production and technical manipulation to bypass filters or trick viewers.”

Examples of violators include channels that “use the exact same background music and repetitive AI generated imagery across many videos, with each video reading out an AI-generated script.”

This policy sounds good, but YouTube’s attempts to curb AI slop (even as Google dumps enough money into the AI bubble to keep itself in the red for the last two quarters) have backlashed onto genuine creators.

A number of creators–like stop-motion channel Tiny Grandma–have had their content incorrectly flagged as AI and demonetized. The folks behind Tiny Grandma reached out to us here at Tubefilter when YouTube stripped their monetization, and we were able to connect them directly with humans at YouTube. But what about creators who can’t get the hookup, and instead are stuck with automated responses?

This new process might help address that concern–but that’s if the process becomes widely used.

Team YouTube does mention that the 30-day grace period is an experiment, and that only certain channels are eligible. We don’t know what the eligibility requirements are–and, as always with YouTube experiments, it’s possible this one won’t ever make it to live service for all users. Seems like it would be nice if it did, though.

Generative media inference startup fal raises $1.3B at a $16B valuation

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Generative media inference startup fal today announced it has raised $1.3 billion in new funding at a valuation of $16 billion, according to Bloomberg. The company will spend the money on computing power and on hiring beyond its current 185 employees.

Fal provides a cloud platform for running image, video and audio generation models, and developers reach all of them through a single application programming interface. Entertainment studios, advertising agencies and game developers are among the customers using those models in production, according to the company.

In-house researchers also post-train some of those models to run faster. Over the past year that work has centered on video, in partnership with the open- and closed-source developers that build the models. Some of the deals pay fal licensing fees or a share of revenue, Bloomberg reported.

Co-founder and Chief Executive Burkay Gur told the outlet the round is as large as it is because demand has surged and serving it takes more compute. “We hit a point in summer where we were not able to serve our customers and had to rate limit them,” he said. Pre-training a model of fal’s own is not on the near-term agenda, said Gur, who did not rule it out for later.

Annualized revenue at fal passed $800 million earlier this year, a person familiar with the matter told Bloomberg. The valuation puts fal just behind rival inference provider Fireworks AI Inc., which closed $1.5 billion in funding at a $17.5 billion valuation in July. Several people familiar with the finances of inference companies told TechCrunch that gross margins across the sector remain thin because compute is expensive to buy or lease.

Gur started the company in 2021 with Chief Technology Officer Gorkem Yurtseven. His resume includes leading machine learning work at Coinbase Global Inc. and an engineering role at Oracle Corp. Yurtseven was previously a developer at Amazon.com Inc.

Bessemer Venture Partners led the Series F round, and its partner Talia Goldberg is joining the board of Features & Labels Inc., as fal is formally known. Nvidia Corp., Sound Ventures, HOF Capital and T. Rowe Price Group Inc. also participated. Fal described Nvidia as a strategic investor.

Today’s announcement also covered an April round led by Sequoia Capital. That Series E round raised $350 million at an $8 billion valuation, per Bloomberg, and fal said the two rounds come to $1.65 billion combined. Last December, the company raised $140 million at a $4.5 billion valuation.

Investors have now put more than $1.9 billion into the company.

Final Fantasy 7 Remake And Rebirth Are Absurdly Cheap On Switch 2 Right Now

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Final Fantasy VII RebirthFinal Fantasy VII RemakeSaleSwitch 2

Final Fantasy VII Remake Intergrade and Rebirth both came to Switch 2 earlier this year and they are currently just $8 each. That is a ridiculously low price for two very good ports of two excellent RPGs.

Rebirth is normally $50 and Remake is normally $40, but until November 17, Square Enix has made the digital versions of the Switch 2 ports available for $7.50 and $8, respectively. Why is Remake priced $0.50 higher than the newer, bigger game? I have no idea. The physical versions are both game key cards as well, so you’re not missing out on anything there.

If you own a Switch 2 and haven’t yet picked these up, I’d definitely recommend it, especially ahead of Final Fantasy VII Revelation, which will close out the trilogy when it arrives in April 2027. Both games perform decently enough on Nintendo’s newer hardware, too.

Square Enix put in a lot of work to get the gorgeous games running at a solid 30fps framerate without completely downgrading the resolution to hell, though Cloud’s spiky hair still looks way better on the other consoles.

If you already own the series on PS5, Xbox Series X/S, or PC, it’s not worth picking up extra versions for the portability factor. There’s no nifty cross-save function like with Cyberpunk 2077. But for people who only have Switch 2s, these are easy pick-ups and will probably never be cheaper.

Scotland lay out Scott McTominay plans as Sebastien Pocognoli awaits first sight of talisman

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Scotland manager Sebastien Pocognoli intends to try to make “a good connection” with Scott McTominay after naming the Napoli midfielder in his squad for the first time.

Pocognoli is relieved the midfielder, who underwent a small heart procedure earlier this season, has returned to the fold.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

The Belgian is looking forward to meeting McTominay in the flesh. It’s already tempting to suggest that Pocognoli is pinning his hopes on the player following an underwhelming start to his time as manager.

The Belgian succeeded Steve Clarke in August. Scotland have since won just one of their first four Nations League games and are now sitting third in Group B1, behind Switzerland and Slovenia.

They require a minimum of four points against North Macedonia at Hampden next Friday and then away to Switzerland three days later to have any chance of finishing second and featuring in the play-offs.

The latter game is two days before the first anniversary of Scotland’s titanic 4-2 win over Denmark that secured a place at this summer’s World Cup. McTominay got that famous night underway with an overhead kick after just three minutes.

He has already scored twice for Napoli since returning to the side last month. Pocognoli has only spoken to McTominay online so far and is looking forward to sitting down with him in person as he looks to establish a connection with Scotland’s most valuable player.

He stressed that it is up to him as manager to find a way to ensure the other players give McTominay the chance to excel rather than simply waiting for the Italy-based star to spark the team. It will be interesting to see who he replaces. John McGinn, Lewis Ferguson and Billy Gilmour were the middle three in the last outing against Slovenia, which Scotland lost 2-1. Ryan Christie played just behind Oli McBurnie in attack.

“He (McTominay) will bring experience, quality, an expectation of goals, a presence in the box - and he’ll bring leadership as part of the captain’s group,” said Pocognoli.

“He has a strong voice. He’s a different character to the others, everyone is different. But it’s up to me to make sure the game plan for him and the players allows him to shine. And it’s not just on him to make the team shine, if you know what I mean? That’s the challenge.”

“I just want to know him a little bit more, from the personal aspect,” he added. “We all know his quality. I think he can fit into the way we want to play.

“So I’m just excited to see him on the pitch, to see if he can bring something extra in the way we want to play.

“Also, to see how he fits into the group with my own eyes. I want to make a real contact with him because before it has only been on the phone or by video call. I’m looking forward to meeting him.”

Pocognoli, who has also brought striker Che Adams back into the squad after injury, stressed that McTominay remains committed to the Scotland cause. The 29-year-old was heavily criticised in the summer following a perceived poor showing in the World Cup, although this was before the problem with his heart came to light.

“He was really disappointed to miss the first camp and was already talking about the next camp,” said Pocognoli, recalling his first conversations with the player.

“At that point we weren’t sure because we had to see his rehabilitation. But he was excited to try his best to reach the November camp and he’s still excited now, which is good.”

Binance relied on AI to fight fraud before hackers exploited internal tool in $6 billion theft

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Attackers exploited an internal AI system to reach wallet engineers, prompting the exchange to seek security partnerships with leading AI companies.

Hackers exploited Binance's internal artificial intelligence tools to compromise engineers before stealing $6.34 billion in cryptocurrency, the exchange disclosed.

In its Nov. 3 security report, Binance said attackers used a compromised employee computer and an internal AI tool on Oct. 20 to take control of two engineers responsible for its wallet infrastructure.

The attackers subsequently obtained access to a container deployment permission token, which helped them penetrate the exchange's wallet-signing operations on Oct. 29 and initiate unauthorized transactions across several blockchain networks.

The disclosure puts AI at the center of one of the largest cryptocurrency thefts on record, adding to concerns about how increasingly connected AI systems can introduce new vulnerabilities into corporate infrastructure.

Binance's investigation does not establish that an AI model autonomously planned or executed the theft. Rather, the attackers exploited an internal AI tool as part of a broader campaign involving stolen credentials, compromised developer software, and social engineering.

In June, a Binance engineer downloaded a tampered software installer while attempting to install a popular AI coding tool. Binance detected the suspicious activity, replaced the device and rotated associated credentials, although information about the employee and colleagues had already been exposed.

In September, another engineer fell victim to a fake recruitment operation involving staged job interviews and a malicious coding assignment. The malware compromised the employee's computer and gave the attackers access, which they later used to move deeper into Binance's systems.

The FBI has attributed the broader attack to North Korea, whose hacking operations have increasingly targeted software developers and cryptocurrency businesses.

The Binance incident follows months of warnings from cybersecurity researchers that artificial intelligence is expanding both hackers' capabilities and the systems available for exploitation.

In February, Google's Mandiant reported that North Korean hackers targeting the cryptocurrency industry had incorporated AI-assisted techniques into their operations.

The researchers documented an intrusion involving a fake video conference, malicious software, and a suspected AI-generated video impersonating a corporate executive. They could not independently confirm the deepfake in that particular incident but identified the group's use of AI tools for reconnaissance and social engineering.

By September, Google's Threat Intelligence Group reported that attackers were moving beyond using AI to generate malicious content toward automated operations that could execute multiple stages of an intrusion.

In one case, hackers compromised a cloud resource and organized an AI-assisted mass credential-harvesting campaign in less than six hours. Google also identified attackers attempting to manipulate AI coding assistants and security scanners during software supply-chain compromises.

The findings closely resemble one aspect of the Binance breach: developers and the AI-powered tools they depend on are becoming targets attackers use to reach sensitive systems.

Separately, AI developer Anthropic disclosed in September that criminals and suspected state-sponsored operators had used its Claude models to conduct reconnaissance, develop exploitation tools, and automate data theft.

Some operations involved AI agents carrying out multiple stages of an attack with limited human supervision, reducing the technical expertise and manpower traditionally required.

The exchange is now seeking support from the same industry whose technologies were featured in the attack.

Binance said it is working to establish a security research fund and develop targeted initiatives with leading AI companies, blockchain industry participants and cybersecurity firms.

The proposed collaboration will focus on strengthening AI security in blockchain environments, although the exchange has not disclosed the fund's proposed size, participating companies or launch date.

It also plans to expand spending on AI-enabled threat detection, endpoint protection, developer-environment security and behavioral anomaly monitoring.

The exchange said it has already rebuilt the affected internal AI tool and wallet infrastructure, replaced compromised employee devices and strengthened access controls.

Binance has also commissioned an independent cybersecurity review to validate its investigation and identify additional safeguards.

The company said approximately $2.85 billion of the $6.34 billion in affected digital assets has been frozen or intercepted, while 98% of impacted token assets had been replenished as of Oct. 31.

The independent review has yet to establish the full extent of the weaknesses that allowed attackers to exploit the exchange's internal AI environment. Its findings will inform the security changes Binance plans to implement as it develops the proposed industry fund.

Where to Find the Small Worlds Most Photographers Walk Past

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Where to Find the Small Worlds Most Photographers Walk Past

Peter Baumgarten was looking for centimeter-tall mushrooms when he spotted a cluster of orange caps on a rotting log. A few warm, rainy days had encouraged mushrooms to emerge in Canada’s McLean’s Park, a forest on Ontario’s Manitoulin Island where he regularly walks his dog. At six feet two inches tall, Baumgarten could easily walk past mushrooms that small if he weren’t specifically looking for them.

Full disclosureThis article was brought to you by OM SYSTEM.

Peter Baumgarten is just one of a number of OM SYSTEM ambassadors who specialize in macro photography and shared with PetaPixel their favorite places to look for small subjects and the encounters that keep them returning time and time again. For Lisa and Tom Cuchara, that includes the meadows and woodland around Little River in southern Maine. Chris McGinnis searches the rainforests of Costa Rica and Panama, while also expanding his macro photography portfolio in Maine and his own backyard.

Their photographs show the miniature world that can turn up on a rotting log or a garden stem. Baumgarten gets low on the forest floor to photograph mushrooms and the slugs that feed on them. The Cucharas and McGinnis have both made some of their favorite finds after dark, the Cucharas in a garden in the Northeast and McGinnis at a pond in Panama.

In one of Baumgarten’s favorite macro photographs, a garden slug stretches along rotting wood beneath a cluster of orange mushrooms, its head raised toward one of the caps.

“What excited me the most was the garden slug getting ready to chow down, which isn’t a common shot,” he says.

Baumgarten takes many of his macro shots at a park close to home. McLean’s Park has several kilometers of trails through mixed forest, which he uses for hiking, biking, cross-country skiing, snowshoeing, and photography.

“It is about a 15-minute drive from my home,” he says. “I go there several times a week to walk my dog. As a mature forest there are plenty of fallen trees and branches that make for the perfect environment for a bunch of different types of mushrooms.”

“What I love about the park is the variety of photographs I can take there,” he notes. “One day I might photograph a person walking their dog in autumn along a leaf-covered trail, with tall trees and yellow foliage all around. Another day, I might be down among the fallen logs and branches photographing mushrooms.”

Baumgarten likes to shoot most of his mushroom macro photos below eye level. “I love capturing them from underneath,” he explains. “For me, the gill structure is perhaps the most interesting and photogenic part of the mushroom. I want a soft background that doesn’t pull focus from my subject. By shooting from just below the mushroom I can usually include the more distant, lush greenery of the forest.”

“While mushrooms don’t move, when I’m photographing a slug in the low light of the forest, I have to be aware that even its slow movement can cause blur,” he continues. “Shooting handheld, I might use a shutter speed of around 1/100 second to keep it sharp, raising the ISO if I need to.”

Bumping up the ISO, the camera’s sensitivity to light, lets him shoot at a faster shutter speed when the light on the forest floor is dim. With a mushroom, he can usually keep the ISO low and slow the shutter down instead. The image stabilization in his OM SYSTEM OM-3 lets him handhold exposures as long as half a second, even while using in-camera focus stacking, which combines the sharp areas of photographs focused at different distances to keep more of a close-up subject in focus.

“I capture mushrooms in a very similar way to how I photograph wildlife,” Baumgarten says. “For many wildlife shots I prefer to use my long lens. This gives me great close-up images of my subject. But a great close-up is not always the best way to showcase the animal. Showing the animal in its environment helps me tell more of the story about where it lives. That’s when I turn to using a wider lens. I will often do the same when photographing mushrooms.”

Mushrooms can appear quickly and may not stay in good condition for long, so Baumgarten recommends watching the weather to help decide when to go looking.

“Mushrooms need warm, moist conditions, so I recommend exploring one or two days after a good rain,” he advises. “I have had great success from late spring to late autumn. I recommend avoiding bright sunny days. The dappled sunlight on the forest floor can work for some shots, but often it is more of a hindrance than a help.”

A subject that stays put gives photographers new to macro more time to experiment with focus and composition.

“Photographing mushrooms is a great way to get into macro shooting,” he adds. “I love the geometry of most mushrooms and the infinite patience they have. I’ve never had a mushroom run away from me as I sneak up on it.”

Those quiet searches are also part of why he keeps returning to the forest.

“I often turn to macro photography to get me out of a rut,” Baumgarten says. “I primarily shoot landscapes and wildlife and over the years there have been many stretches of time where I just couldn’t capture shots that I was happy with. It’s at these times, when I feel a bit down, that I grab my macro lens and head into the forest for a bit of exploration and de-stressing. I slow down, observe carefully, and invariably find something in the macro world to photograph. And the best part is, I don’t have to go far. Macro subjects are everywhere, and a local park or garden can be a great place to start looking. I’m lucky to have McLean’s Park just a short drive from home.”

For Lisa and Tom Cuchara, some of the strangest subjects turn up after dark.

“One evening I headed into the garden to look for tiny nighttime wonders,” Lisa Cuchara recalls. “Imagine my surprise when I found a bright green hornworm sporting what looked like dozens of tiny white marshmallows. Those little white blobs weren’t eggs or fungus, though. They were the silken cocoons of tiny parasitoid wasps. The wasp larvae had developed inside the living caterpillar before coming out to spin their cocoons. It was creepy and fascinating at the same time.”

Gardeners don’t like hornworms, since a hungry one can make a massive amount of tomato foliage disappear overnight. The wasps, most likely a Cotesia species, are another matter.

“This was a perfect example of nature’s own pest control,” Lisa explains. “Unlike regular parasites, which generally don’t kill their hosts, parasitoids kill the animal they develop in. These tiny wasps help keep hornworm numbers down, which makes them real allies for gardeners.”

“When I saw this scene, I knew that the challenge was getting the detail of both the caterpillar and those wonderfully wonky white cocoons while working handheld in the dark,” Lisa recalls. “Even at f/11, there just isn’t enough depth of field to get everything sharp in a single exposure.”

An aperture of f/11 is a small lens opening that would normally extend depth of field, the zone of a photograph that looks sharp. In-camera focus stacking, used with the Cucharas’ OM-1 Mark II and the M.Zuiko Digital ED 90mm F3.5 Macro IS PRO lens, got around the problem.

“I was able to shoot a focus-stacked image handheld, without setting up a tripod or disturbing my subject,” Lisa notes.

For insects, the Cucharas use a small flash fitted with a diffuser.

“A bare flash gives you harsh highlights and deep shadows, especially on an insect’s shiny exoskeleton,” the Cucharas explain. “Using a diffuser turns that small flash into a much larger, softer light compared to a tiny subject. The flash also helps freeze movement, whether it’s a slight breeze moving a leaf or an insect shifting position.”

“Without a flashlight and a willingness to explore the garden after sunset, I might have walked right past an extraordinary biological story unfolding just inches away,” Lisa adds.

On other outings, the Cucharas have photographed a moth laying eggs at night and a crab spider grabbing an unsuspecting bumblebee. They’ve also watched a caterpillar that had just shed its outer skin, known as a molt.

“A black swallowtail caterpillar will eat its own molt for energy,” they add. “Those spontaneous moments of seeing something so unique are just one reason we enjoy macro photography so much.”

The Cucharas have turned their own yard into a wildlife habitat, replacing most of the lawn with the goal of mainly native plants grown without pesticides or herbicides. They also lead macro and close-up photography retreats at Little River in North Berwick, Maine.

“We love places where you can move just a few feet and find a whole different world,” they say.

Insects are cold-blooded, so how active they are depends on the temperature and conditions around them.

“Pick a rainy, drizzly, or overcast day,” the Cucharas advise. “Insects like to hang out under the leaves and they’re less likely to move. And get there early, because a lot of insects are cold and slow in the early hours, which gives you more time to photograph them.”

“Slow down and look under every leaf,” they continue. “Don’t rush from flower to flower; pick a small area and explore it from a few angles. You start to see a jumping spider on a stalk, holes in a leaf from insects eating, or a curled leaf with a caterpillar inside.”

“If you’re just starting to practice macro photography, a stationary flower with some personality, pollen, or water droplets is a wonderful starting point,” the Cucharas note. “A flower past its prime, a crooked stem, a curled petal, or an oddly shaped mushroom can be a far more compelling story than a flawless blossom. And learn to notice the background, because it’s just as important as the subject.”

“At one of our retreats, six groups rotated through my session to photograph a swallowtail chrysalis on its host plant,” Lisa recalls. “It gave me a chance to talk about fennel and other host plants, and about how to photograph it without disturbing the spot or making it more obvious to predators. No photograph is worth possible harm to the subject, including insects and the plants they’re on.”

Lisa also says that it’s not necessary to have a large piece of land to start creating your own macro photography sanctuary. “I always recommend planting more native and host plants everywhere, even if it’s just a few planters at your condo. Native plants are what attract the insects that look fascinating in a photograph.”

Even with their own garden, the Cucharas still head out to other spots for macro photography. However, unlike static landscape overlooks, macro photography is always delivering something new. “We often visit the same places again and again, and we always come home with different photographs,” the Cucharas explain. “Flowers open and fade, insect species change from month to month, and the light and weather change. Macro photography might be the best genre for photographers who are always looking to shoot, and learn, something new.”

Chris McGinnis has captured some of his favorite macro photographs after dark, in the rainforests of Costa Rica and Panama.

McGinnis says that one of the most interesting subjects in the Central American rainforest is the red-eyed tree frog. “With these frogs, I am constantly surprised with the variety of moments I am able to capture them in, and maybe my favorite moment was on a trip to Panama with fellow OM SYSTEM ambassador Lee Hoy,” McGinnis remembers. “We had some workshop clients with us, and they faded one by one and went off to sleep, but Lee and I stayed out shooting. We were on this little pond, looking for frogs, and we’d found and photographed several. All of a sudden Lee says, ‘Red-eyed tree frogs in amplexus.’ I said, ‘What? What do you mean?’, not knowing what amplexus meant. Lee pointed to a branch and there were two red-eyed tree frogs with the male on top of the female. I’ve photographed hundreds of these frogs before, but I had never seen them do this. The shot was a unique addition to my portfolio, and I learned a new word in the process.”

According to McGinnis, red-eyed tree frogs spend the day higher up in the trees and come down toward the water as the sun goes down, which is why he looks for them at night. But whether it’s day or night, he often chooses to shoot macro photographs with a non-macro lens.

“That night with the frogs was a time when I could have used my M.Zuiko Digital ED 90mm F3.5 Macro IS PRO,” he recalls. “I wanted to be up really close, but I wanted a wider view so the story was the frogs in their environment, as that can make a more interesting shot.”

Instead, he had the M.Zuiko Digital ED 8-25mm F4.0 PRO on his camera that night. It’s a wide-angle zoom that can focus close enough to fill the frame with a frog while still showing the branch the pair was sitting on.

“I don’t think I could ever get tired of searching for red-eyed tree frogs,” he adds. “It’s an addiction for me. I find one, I photograph it until it jumps away or I’ve exhausted every angle I can get. Then I could go back to my room and go to sleep, or I could try to find another one. And I have a really hard time walking away, because that next one might be something I’ve never seen before, like capturing two red-eyed tree frogs in amplexus.”

“The biggest challenge of the rainforest at night is getting lost,” McGinnis warns. “So I always get a guide, as there is incredible value in going out with someone who knows their way around the rainforest. Not to mention the danger hiding on the ground and in the trees. Central America has lots of venomous snakes. You grab the wrong thing, you step in the wrong place, and you could be in trouble. A local guide knows how to keep you from accidentally running into one of them.”

Rain is a given in a rainforest. “Photographing in the rain is challenging because it can be harder to find subjects or keep your clothing dry, but luckily I never have to worry about my camera gear in the rain, which eliminates one of the challenges,” he notes. With IP53-rated weather sealing, his OM-1 Mark II can keep shooting through snow, dust, and rain. “I’ve been shooting days on end in pouring, torrential rain in Central America, and I’ve never had a problem with my gear.”

When McGinnis isn’t finding small subjects in the Central American rainforest, he also helps lead multi-day macro retreats with Lisa and Tom Cuchara and other OM SYSTEM ambassadors, on fellow ambassador Steve Morello’s property in southern Maine, as well as other locations. One of his favorite subjects on Morello’s property is dragonflies.

“If you find a dragonfly as the sun’s coming up, they’re not warm yet,” McGinnis advises. “They are cold and they might have dew on their wings, making it harder for them to fly. They’re almost like sitting ducks at that time, and much easier to photograph than in the heat of the day, when they’re busy hunting.”

He also searches his own backyard after dark, where wolf spiders and other hunting spiders give themselves away. Their eyes have a reflective layer that bounces a flashlight beam straight back, a glint known as eyeshine.

“In my own backyard, I love to photograph spiders and praying mantises after dark,” he continues. “Spiders hunt at night, and they are easier to find than most people realize. If you hold your flashlight up to the side of your head and scan the grass about 10 to 15 feet ahead of you, the eyeballs of a spider will flicker back at you. In the pitch black, all you’re looking for is those little diamonds in the grass. Having the flashlight right to the side of your eye keeps your line of sight in line with the beam of the flashlight.”

Some of McGinnis’s favorite discoveries don’t happen until he’s back at his computer. “I was trying to get this mantis to cooperate, and I ended up with tons of shots of it in different poses.”

“As I was reviewing them, I noticed a little something else in the shot that was invisible to my naked eye,” he continues. “That mite on its head is very, very tiny, just one or two millimeters. That’s actually happened to me many times, and finding those tiny surprises when I zoom in on the computer is one of the best things about macro photography.”

McGinnis says this is a great way for both beginners and seasoned macro photography veterans to practice and capture unique photos. “Go outside with a flashlight and scan the yard, and you’re bound to find something. No matter where you live, especially in the summertime, go out after dark with your camera and a light. On a nice, warm summer night, it’s just really fun. I’m exploring this small world, and through that exploration, new things reveal themselves. That’s what keeps me going back. Even if it’s my backyard, a local park, or a tropical rainforest, I’m always discovering new things and finding interesting stories in the small world that most other photographers walk by.”
